2.1.6.3 Energy

2.1.6.3.1 Rajiv Gandhi Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana (RGGVY)

Rajiv Gandhi Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana (RGGVY) was launched by Gol
in April 2005 to provide electricity to all rural households within a period of
five years. The scheme is implemented with 90 per cent subsidy from Gol.
Electrification of below poverty line (BPL) rural households is fully financed
by Gol. The West Bengal State Electricity Distribution Company Limited
(WBSEDCL) is the implementing agency. During 2006-11, out of the
available funds of € 99.54 crore, हैं 71.16 crore (72 per cent) was expended.

As per DPR prepared by WBSEDCL, the aim was to (i) electrify 185 virgin
mouzas and (ii) energise 1.75 lakh rural households including 1.09 lakh BPL
households in 1346 mouzas by March 2010.

As of March 2011, with the electrification of 185 virgin mouzas, all the
mouzas in the District were electrified. However, against the target of
energising 1.75 lakh rural households in 1346 mouzas, achievement was mere
0.21 lakh households (12 per cent) in 392 mouzas. This included 0.19 lakh
BPL households which accounted for 18 per cent of the targeted 1.09 lakh.
Thus, the targets were not met as of March 2011 and the objective of
electrifying all rural households remained unachieved. WBSEDCL attributed
non-achievement of targets to delay in procurement of materials and non-
mobilisation of manpower.

Further, out of 659 mouzas and 0.33 lakh BPL households where
electrification work was completed, 267 mouzas (41 per cent) and 0.18 lakh
BPL households (46 per cent) were not energised. This was attributed by

45


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

WBSEDCL to lack of co-ordination among its various wings and apathy of
WBSEDCL towards energisation of BPL consumers.

As per Rural Electricity Policy (2006), a village would be declared as
electrified based on the certificate issued by GP. It was noticed that out of 577
mouzas energised in Dakshin Dinajpur, 244 (43 per cent) were not declared as
electrified for want of certificates from GP. Thus, though all the mouzas in the
District were electrified as per WBSEDCL records, they were not declared as
electrified. WBSEDCL attributed this to delay in collection of GP certificates
by contractor.

Audit noted the following irregularities in course of scrutiny:

>

v

Under the scheme, BPL households were to be provided the connection
free of cost. Joint inspections (with Gram Pradhan) of five” mouzas in
three selected blocks where the scheme was implemented under the
Tenth Plan (up to March 2007), however, revealed that 52 out of 53 (98
per cent) BPL households had to pay हैं 15010 हैं 200 for Pressed Cement
Concrete (PCC) pole for service connection. It was seen that in Eleventh
Plan® (2007-12), BPL households were provided connection free of cost
using ‘U’ shaped service pipe instead of PCC pole. Audit noted that BPL
beneficiaries had to pay money for faulty planning of WBSEDCL.

For creation of Rural Electricity Distribution Backbone WBSEDCL
awarded (August 2006) work for construction of one sub-station at
Rampur in Tapan Block at € 3.11 crore to a contractor. The sites were,
however, handed over (September 2007) 13 months after the award.
Further, WBSEDCL had to change the design and Bill of Quantity™ and
delayed approval of the drawings which was indicative of inadequate
planning. Thus, the sub-station slated to be completed by August 2007
got delayed by 22 months depriving the beneficiaries of quality power
for 22 months. Further, work for erection of 1279 KM of three phases
and 131 KM of single phase LT lines was awarded to another contractor
in February 2009. Against the same, 260 KM (20 per cent) of three
phase lines and 440 KM (336 per cent) single phase lines have been
extended as of March 2011. Abnormal extension of single phase line
disproportionate with three phase lines resulted in heavy load demand
leading to frequent burning of Distribution Transformers (DTRs) and
power interruptions frustrating the objective.

RGGVY is implemented by the Rural Electrification (RE) wing of
WBSEDCL. After completion of electrification work of 185 virgin
mouzas, excess material worth हैं 1.30 crore was returned by contractors
between June and September 2009. WBSEDCL transferred the excess
materials, which should have been part of the RE store, to its O&M
division. This was tantamount to diversion of scheme fund of
₹ 1.30 crore.

2.1.7 Monitoring

[2.4.7.1 Supervision

Guidelines for implementation of the Central and State plan schemes stipulate
monitoring requirements. However, the DM is responsible for monitoring the
overall progress of implementation of various developmental programmes and
ensuring that these are executed within the specified timeframe and approved
budget. In view of convergence of multiplicity of developmental schemes and
involvement of larger number of implementing agencies, stronger and
comprehensive district level monitoring mechanism needs to be in place.
However, in the absence of any such consolidated monitoring mechanism at
the district level, sufficiency of monitoring was not assessable against
standards set. However, that DPC convened only four review meetings during
2006-11, no meeting having been convened in 2007-08 and 2010-11, speaks
of the gaps in monitoring. Lack of monitoring impacted execution of schemes
significantly, as discussed in specific cases.

2.1.8 Conclusion

While audit observed some positive trends in the Social Sector programmes
relating to health and education, there are quite a few areas that have been
flagged for focused attention of the State/District administration.

The overall impact of various socio-economic developmental programmes
being implemented in Dakshin Dinajpur District on the living standards of the
people of the District was limited as indicated by non-achievement of targets.
The district either failed to formulate district plans or formulated these with
such delay that the exercise proved to be futile. The individual planning of
major schemes reviewed were also deficient. While PRIs are empowered to
prepare specific plans for integrated development of their area, lack of
structured annual action plans at these levels resulted in their inability to
expend funds provided to them for implementation of various programmes.
Absence of adequate participation from PRIs in the planning process is
hindering the planned progress of the District and the felt needs at the grass
root level remain unaddressed.

Under the Health sector, though Implementation of NRHM brought about
some positive effect in terms of wider coverage of institutional deliveries and
reduction in infant mortality, there is much scope for improvement as regards
setting up of new health centres and supplementing deficient facilities in
inpatient services like, operation theatres, labour room, pathological test
facility etc. Besides, significant shortages of medical officers and paramedics
were also a matter of concern, which potentially affected quality of health care
services. Functioning of Rogi Kalyan Samitis needs to be honed up for
ensuring better community participation and ownership in health delivery
services.

48


Chapter-2-Performance Audit

There was no increase in the number of elementary schools during the last five
years. Even the existing schools lacked requisite infrastructure like stipulated
number of class rooms, toilets, etc. as various interventions of the Scheme
were either not implemented or inadequately implemented. Disparities in
posting of teachers coupled with deficiencies in capacity building of teachers
might potentially affect the quality of teaching. Though drop-out and
promotion rate of students improved, enrollment of children decreased during
last five years.

ICDS programme failed to provide requisite nutrition to the targeted
population for 300 days in a year mainly due to shortage in supply of food.
Besides, not only the Anganwadi Centres (AW centres) suffered from
inadequate infrastructural facilities, supervision on their functioning was also
compromised owing to shortages of CDPOs.

Under Water supply sector, out of 24 works taken up under ARWSP during
2006-2011, two schemes due for completion in December 2010, were yet to be
completed as of July 2011, while two other schemes had not been taken up
due to land dispute and insufficiency of water sources. Slow implementation
of work resulted in safe drinking water not being supplied to school children
in quality affected area and 8.83 lakh people of fluoride affected areas. In
violation of CE’s order, Bacteriological test of the existing 40 PWSSs was not
conducted at all since July 2009. Remaining two schemes were ongoing as of
July 2011.

Even after implementation of PMGSY for last ten years, 52 per cent of the
habitations remained unconnected, though length of road increased by 73 per
cent and number of connected habitations by 64 per cent.

The components of JNNURM like sanitation and construction of houses were
restricted due to irregular supply of building material and encroachment of
land.

Employment generation schemes like SGSY and NREGS failed to make the
desired impact in the District and suffered from lax supervision. Concerted
efforts need to be taken to exercise effective oversight in order to achieve the
targets.

Though the RGGVY succeeded in bringing electricity to all the mouzas in the
District, all rural households in the District could not be energised after five
years of launching the programme.

The State Government will have to address these serious issues in order to
ensure development of Dakshin Dinajpur District at the desired pace.

The matter was referred to the Government in September 2011; reply is,
however, awaited (November 2011).

49


Chapter-2-Performance Audit

2.1.9 Summary of recommendations

Under NRHM, the District Health Society should commission
household and facility surveys to assess the requirement and identify
the gaps in health care infrastructure in the District. Initiatives
should be taken for time-bound construction of Sub-centres to equip
the health centres with facilities and manpower as per IPHS norms,
to achieve the Programme objectives.

Under SSA, the civil works should be geared up to provide the
minimum infrastructural facilities to all schools in a time-bound
manner. Posting of teachers needs to be rationalised to remove
extreme disparities in PTR.

Under ICDS, immediate steps should be taken for improvement of
infrastructural facilities of Anganwadi centres. Provision of adequate
food material should be ensured to provide stipulated nutrition to the
beneficiaries for 300 days in a year.

Annual Action Plan for the Accelerated Rural Water Supply
Programme should be prepared in accordance with the guidelines
indicating the prioritisation of incomplete works particularly the Not
Covered habitations. Monitoring of the schemes and testing the water
quality at prescribed interval should be enforced.

Under Indira Awas Yojana, strict financial control and monitoring to
be enforced to prevent misuse of funds and deprivation of central
funds. The district authorities should analyse reasons for
disproportionate financial and physical progress.

Implementation of Total Sanitation Campaign programme needs to
be geared up for achievement of targets. IEC activities should be
strengthened.

Under Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana, financial controls
should be made stringent to avoid excess payments. Also, efforts
should be made to complete works within stipulated time.

Steps need be taken for effective utilisation of infrastructure created
under NREGS and SGSY. Concerted efforts needed to be taken for
achieving the targets as per labour budget and thereby ensuring
100 days employment under NREGS.

Activities of concerned wings of West Bengal State Electricity
Distribution Corporation Limited should be co-ordinated for effective
implementation of the Rajiv Gandhi Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

URBAN DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT

2.2 Working of Siliguri Jalpaiguri Development Authority

Executive Summary

Located at the narrow corridor linking North-Eastern States to the rest of the
country and on the traditional trade route to Sikkim, Nepal, Bhutan and mandated
with the responsibilities of planned and integrated development of Siliguri
Jalpaiguri Planning Area (SJPA), Siliguri Jalpaiguri Development Authority
(SIDA) was constituted in April 1980 under the West Bengal Town and Country
(Planning and Development) Act, 1979. Since then, it has taken leadership in
providing connectivity with the existing state and national highways by way of
constructing new and alternative bye-roads, fly-overs and bridges and has
executed a number of projects for development of housing and transport
infrastructure. During 2006-11, 481 developmental works valuing % 256 crore
were undertaken by SJDA, of which 177 were completed up to March 2011. It has
a total notified planning area of 5.75 lakh acres under its jurisdiction and prepared
long term Master plan for land use in the form of Land Use and Development
Control Plan (LUDCP) for 1.35 lakh acres. Audit has flagged certain areas of
concern as regards planned use of land, strengthening financial and operational
controls, acquisition, utilisation of land, infrastructure development and internal
control.

> Contrary to its statutory responsibility to prepare Land Use and Development
Control Plan within two years of notification, SIDA did not do so in respect of
substantial part of its notified area after seven to 31 years of notifications. As
a result, individual development works taken up by SJDA did not have desired
linkage with long term development plans.

>» Budget management was flawed as would be evident from instances of delays
in sending budget proposals to Government and wide variation between
budget and actuals. Financial management was deficient leading to shortfall in
collection of toll charges, rents, etc. and retention of heavy cash balances in
savings and current bank accounts.

> Inability to protect its interest was apparent from the instances of
non-registration of 462.43 acres of land acquired, acquisition of excess land
than requisitioned by the requiring body, transfer of 8.58 acres of land in
excess of acquisition to a requiring body free of cost, etc. noticed in audit.
Besides, 48.748 acres of land acquired at %7.83 crore was under
encroachments, indicating lack of diligence.

> In the absence of comprehensive policy for fixation of prices for leasing out of
land, decisions taken by SJDA were often arbitrary resulting in loss of
revenue on allotment of land at reduced rate and extension of undue favour to
lessees. Instances of execution of works outside SJPA were noticed.



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

> The control deficiencies in SIDA were compounded by lax monitoring by
Urban Development Department, though the Act had mandated the
Department to monitor the activities of the Authority and Government was
represented in the board of the Authority by Minister and Principal Secretary
of the Department.

2.2.1 Introduction

Siliguri Jalpaiguri Development Authority (SJDA) was constituted in April 1980
under the West Bengal Town and Country (Planning and Development) Act, 1979
(the Act) with the main objective of ensuring planned and integrated development
of Siliguri Jalpaiguri Planning Area (SJPA) by providing facilities like land,
roads, drainage system, housing complexes, industrial estates, marketing
complexes etc. SJPA comprises total area of 575031 acres notified (up to
February 2011) by the Government of West Bengal (GoWB) in the jurisdiction of
ten Police Stations of Darjeeling and Jalpaiguri Districts.

The Annual accounts of SJDA are prepared through Chartered Accountants, while
audit of the accounts is entrusted under Section 20 (1) of CAG’s DPC Act 1971 to
the Comptroller and Auditor General of India by the Government in pursuance of
Section 111 of the Act ibid. The SIDA has been incurring losses for last four
years up to 2009-10; the accounts for 2010-11 has not yet been finalised.

2.2.2 Organisational set up

The SJDA functions under the administrative control of Urban Development
Department and its activities are managed by a Board! constituted by the GoWB
under Section 11 of the Act, ibid. The Chief Executive Officer (CEO), who is the
Member-Secretary of the Board, supervises day-to-day activities of SIDA. There
are four wings in SJDA. They are Engineering, Planning, Administrative and
Accounts headed by Superintending Engineer, Associate Planner, Assistant
Executive Officer and Accounts Officer respectively, who assist the CEO in their
respective fields. Besides, there are 65 regular officers/staff of different cadres
and 11 personnel under contract service.

The Board of Directors of SJDA is chaired by the Minister in Charge of UD
Department with Principal Secretary of UD Department, Divisional
Commissioner of Jalpaiguri Division, District Magistrates of Darjeeling and
Jalpaiguri as Members. As per the Act, soon after the close of a year, SJDA shall
prepare a report of its activities during the preceding year and submit it to the
State Government.

2.2.3 Audit objectives

The objective of audit was to examine whether

2 Comprising 12 to 14 members including the Chairman, the Minister in Charge of UD Department

52


Chapter-2-Performance Audit

> planning for development of the area was adequate and effective;

> financial management was economical and prudent;

> land acquired by SJDA for different purposes were utilised fruitfully
and leasing of land was transparent, efficient and cost effective;

> schemes taken up for infrastructure development were appropriate and

executed successfully; and

internal controls were adequate and functioning of SJDA was
adequately monitored.

2.2.4 Audit criteria

Audit criteria used for the study were as under:

> Norms and procedures laid down in West Bengal Town and Country
(Development and Planning) Act, 1979 and Development Authority
(Audit and Accounts) Rules, 1981 (DA Rules);

> Guidelines of individual schemes framed by Government of India and
executed by SJDA;

> Monitoring mechanism formulated by the State;

> Land Acquisition procedure followed by Land and Land Reforms

Department; and
> Public Works Code.

2.2.5 Audit coverage and methodology

The performance audit of SIDA was conducted during May-July 2011 covering
activities during the period 2006-2011 through test check of records of the
Authority, including the records relating to 105 works out of 177 works
completed during 2006-11. An entry conference was conducted in July 2011 with
the Principal Secretary, Urban Development Department and with the CEO of
SJDA in May 2011 to ascertain the activities of SIDA and schemes executed
during 2006-2011 and to share with them the audit objectives, audit criteria and
the audit methodology. On completion of audit, the audit observations were
discussed in November 2011 with the Principal Secretary, UD Department in an
exit conference and the views of the Government have been incorporated in
appropriate places. The important audit findings are discussed in the following

2.2.6 Developmental Plan

According to Section 31 of the Act ibid, every Development Authority shall,
within two years of declaration of the planning area and with the approval of the
GoWB, prepare Land Use and Development Control Plan (LUDCP) formulating

53


LUDCP prepared
only for

59 per cent of
Planning area
notified up to
May 2004

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

the policy and the general proposals in respect of the development of land and
indicating broadly the manner in which the land is proposed to be used, such as
residential, commercial, industrial, agricultural, natural scenic beauty, forest,
wild life, natural resources, fishery and landscaping for public and
semi-public open spaces, parks and playgrounds.

The Planning Section of the SJDA is responsible for preparation of LUDCP,
which, after approval by the Board of Directors, was to be forwarded to the UD
Department for approval.

2.2.6.1 Land Use and Development Control Plan

Initially, 51704 comprised (April 1980) an area of 2,87,130.2 acres spread over six
PS viz Bhaktinagar, Jalpaiguri, Matigara, Naxalbari, Rajganj and Siliguri.
Subsequently, 2,87,901.152 acres in four PSs? were added to SJPA between
March 2002 and February 2011. Thus, as of March 2011, the planning area of
SJDA comprised a total of 5,75,031.352 acres in 10 PSs of Darjeeling and
Jalpaiguri districts.

Against 2,87,130 acres of land area available under its jurisdiction in 1980, SIDA
prepared LUDCP for 1,35,262.54 acres (47 per cent), which was approved by
GoWB in August 1992. The LUDCP due for 41488.09 acres of SJPA land
notified between March 2002 and May 2004 was also not prepared as of
November 2011. Thus, LUDCP for 1,93,355.75 acres (59 per cent) of land was
not done even after seven to 31 years of notifications. As a result, SIDA was
unable to assess and identify the areas available for various developmental works
and to check unplanned growth, if any, within its planning area.

The SJDA received grant of € 0.59 crore during November 2005 to March 2009
from GoWB for preparation of LUDCP. The amount remained parked in the
deposit account for 24 months (® 0.59 crore) to 64 months (% 0.03 crore) up to
March 2011, as the works were not taken up. The CEO, SJDA attributed
(July 2011) the same to lack of sufficient in-house qualified planning expertise,
but assured that preparation would be taken up shortly.

The reply did not address the issue of non-preparation of LUDCP for the notified
area within the stipulated period of two years, which was the statutory
responsibility of SJDA. Further, the Act stipulated that in case a Development
Authority fails to prepare the development plan within the stipulated period, the
State Government may direct any of its officers to prepare or cause to be prepared
the same. The UD Department did not take any such step to get the LUDCP done.

The CEO intimated (November 2011) that the Government had consented to
SJDA’s proposal for strengthening the Planning Section. It was further intimated
that SJDA engaged a private agency in June 2006 for preparation of Land Use
Map and Register, which was almost complete as of November 2011. Though,

2.2.7 Financial management

SIDA earns revenue receipts from development charges, bank interest, toll
charges, lease rent of land/ market/ shops, parking fees, etc. Besides, it receives
loans from State Government and grants/ subsidy from State/ Central
Governments for different developmental works and schemes. During the period
2006-10, the quantum of such grants and loans stood at %97.63 crore and
र₹ 9.69 crore respectively. Year-wise position of receipts and expenditure vis-d-vis
budget estimates is given in Table 2.2.1.

2.2.7.1 Delay in submission of budget

According to Section 110 of the Act, ibid, read with DA Rules, 1981, SJDA was
required to submit budget for the next financial year to the Government on or
before 1 November every year, showing estimated receipts and expenditures in
prescribed forms. SJDA intimated that it submitted the budgets to the GoWB for
the financial years 2006-07 and 2007-08 both on 7 April 2008 with delays of 29
months and 17 months respectively, after close of the concerned financial years.
It, however, could not produce the copies of budget estimates to Audit. The dates
of submission of budgets for the financial years 2008-09 to 2010-11 were also not
made available to Audit, though called for. As the estimates of fund requirements
of the SIDA were sent to GoWB only after closure of the relevant years, there
was no linkage between the funds allotted by the GoWB and the schemes
sanctioned/taken up by the SJDA. Scrutiny revealed that due to non-receipt of
funds, eight works with estimated cost of रैं 14.44 crore sanctioned durin,
2008-09 (seven works) and 2010-11 (one work) were not started, while one work
with estimated cost of %2 crore (sanctioned in 2007-08) was abandoned in
2009-10. This was accepted (July 2011) by the CEO.

The laxity in budget preparation resulted in variations in actual receipts and
expenditures as compared to budget estimates of the SJDA during the years
2006-07 to 2009-10. Accounts for 2010-11 has not been prepared
(November 2011).

2.2.7.2 Working results

According to Section 112 of the Act, soon after the close of a year, SJDA shall
prepare a report of its activities during the preceding year and submit it to the
State Government. Further, in terms of Section 111 of the Act, ibid read with
Development Authority (Audit and Accounts) Rules, 1981, SJDA was
required to prepare annual accounts for submission to the Auditor appointed by
the GoWB within | June every year.

It was noticed that SJDA appointed Chartered Accountants for preparation of the
Accounts. The accounts for the years 2006-2010 were, however, submitted for
audit with delays ranging from 160 days to 241 days while the accounts for
2010-11 had not yet (November 2011) been finalised. Government, however,
continued to release funds without receiving the audited accounts. As a result,
timely and proper utilisation of the funds allocated could not be ensured by the
Government. Audited accounts for 2009-10 disclosed accumulated deficit of
% 87.89 crore. This was mainly on account of liability of € 93.86 crore as interest
on Government loans.

The CEO attributed (July 2011) the delay in preparation of accounts mainly to the
time taken for estimation of different provisions and compilation of the same. The
contention was not acceptable as it was a statutory duty to submit the accounts
within 1 June every year and persistent delays weakened the financial
management.

The SJDA suffered continuous losses during 2006-07 to 2009-10; year-wise
losses being % 7.33 crore, % 11.12 crore, % 10.36 crore and % 9.47 crore
respectively. However, test check disclosed instances of avoidable financial
burden of हैं 1.28 crore due to execution of schemes (% 47 lakh) outside its

CEO stated (July 2011) that the Board decided to bear the expenditure on works
outside its planning area for developmental purpose, which was in the interest of
the common people. The reply was not acceptable as SJDA was not statutorily
liable to bear any expenditure on works outside SJPA.

2.2.7.3 Government dues remaining unpaid

Between March 1981 and March 2009, SJDA obtained loans aggregating
% 58.42 crore from GoWB at rates of interest varying from nine per cent to
15 per cent per annum. In terms of the Government orders, the overdue
instalments of loan together with interest amounted to % 115.43 crore’ as of
March 2011. SJDA neither paid interest nor repaid principal to Government till
March 2011. The Department did not take any action for recovery of dues.

CEO stated (July 2011) that SIDA would focus on payment of interest and
repayment of principal taken from GoWB.

2.2.7.4 Maintenance of balance in savings/current accounts

During 2006-11, SIDA maintained eight to nine bank accounts, of which two
were designated for Valmiki Ambedkar Awas Yojana and West Bengal Municipal
Development Fund®. Scrutiny of bank statements and pass books in respect of
seven other accounts revealed retention of minimum monthly balances of
0.94 crore to ¥ 76.95 crore in aggregate. SJDA neither fixed any minimum
amount to be retained in the bank account nor prepared cash flow statement to
assess its requirement of liquid funds for day to day transactions so as to gainfully
invest surplus funds. During 2006-11, SJDA maintained two current accounts
(United Bank of India and State Bank of India) meant for day to day transactions.
These accounts retained minimum balance of € 0.10 lakh to हैं 9.18 crore. The
reasons for maintaining non-interest bearing current accounts was not stated,
though called for.

The CEO stated (July 2011) that minimising the number of bank accounts was
under active consideration. The reply was silent about reasons for maintenance of
substantial cash balance in saving/current accounts. The CEO further intimated
(November 2011) that conversion of current accounts into savings accounts was
under process.

As per Section 69 of Finance Act, 1994 read with Rule 4 of the Service tax Rules,
1994, every person who provided a taxable service of value exceeding हैं 9 lakh in
the preceding financial year is required to recover the service tax from the client

Scrutiny of records revealed that the revenue received by SJDA from rent and

2.2.7.6 Lack of control over collection of revenue

SJDA earned revenue from development charges, lease rent of land/ market/ shop
rooms, toll charges, parking fees, etc. However, it has no policy to fix the reserve
price for tenders for selection of toll collecting agencies. Besides, although the
revenue from the tenants, shop owners and toll collecting agencies remained
outstanding for more than one year to 17 years, SJDA did not enforce recovery
from the defaulters. As a result, aggregate amount of outstanding dues increased
from % 1.08 crore in March 2006 to रैं 1.62 crore in March 2010. Of this,
र₹ 0.37 crore on account of rent of stalls at Surya Sen Market handed over to
Siliguri Mahakuma Parishad, was irrecoverable because of delay in realisation.
The Board decided (October 2010) to waive the amount in view of difficulties
faced by the payees, leading to loss of revenue.

Further, SJDA printed two lakh toll receipts (1.5 lakh in May 2007 and 0.5 lakh in
May 2009) of various denominations’ worth हैं 0.56 crore. Though the same was
shown as issued in the stock register, details of issue were neither on record, nor
intimated by the Authority. It was stated (August 2009) that receipts were utilised
for collection of toll* during the process of selection of regular toll collecting
agency. SJDA did not monitor utilisation of toll receipts and correlate these to the
amount of tolls deposited during that period. Though, SIDA had stated that
collections of toll were deposited regularly, it could not produce any record in
support of amount deposited by the security agency. This indicated lack of
monitoring of revenue collection by SJDA.

2.2.8 Acquisition and leasing of land

As per order of GoWB (May 2011), SJDA prepared a status report of land.
However, the basic records/registers in support of the data included in the Status
Report could not be shown to Audit. As per the statement, SIDA acquired
1207.958 acres of land till March 2011 for % 101.35 crore through Land
Acquisition (LA) Collectors (821.468 acres) of Darjeeling and Jalpaiguri, on lease
from Government Department/Authorities (324.98 acres) and direct purchase
(61.51 acres) from land owners. Out of this 801.189 acres were utilised,
48.748 acres were under encroachment and 237.59 acres involved in litigation.
Time frame for utilisation of remaining 120.431 acres was not fixed.

SJDA did not make available the details of land allotted/ utilised during 2006-11.
As a result, the number of allottees and total quantum of land allotted during this
period along with the purpose vis-d-vis the status of utilisation were not
ascertainable in audit. Moreover, SJDA did not indicate mouza/plot-wise
identification of the land in LUDCP; nor did the Board of SJDA draw any
reference of the LUDCP in the acquisition proposals. As such, adherence to or
deviation from the existing LUDCP could not be examined in audit. Scrutiny
revealed instances of extra expenditure, blockage of funds and loss on leasing of
land as discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

2.2.8.1 Delay in registration of acquired land

Out of 1207.958 acres of land acquired, SJDA was yet to register acquisitions/
lease deeds in respect of 462.43 acres of land acquired under West Bengal Land
Reforms Act, 1955, reasons for which were not on record. In absence of proper
record keeping, it was not possible to assess the delays in registering the deeds. In
absence of registered deeds, SJDA was unable to get the legal title on
462.43 acres of acquired land, indicating lack of seriousness in utilisation of the
acquired land.

2.2.8.2 Extra expenditure on purchase of bulk land

In order to meet the requirement of land adjacent to urban areas, SIDA decided
(June and September 2007) to create a land bank. During the period from
November 2008 to January 2011, SIDA purchased 52.485 acres of land directly
from owners at rates varying from हैं 16.50 lakh to € 30.80 lakh per acre against
market value ranging from = 6.01 lakh to % 19.80 lakh per acre as discussed
below:

20 per cent enhancement (® 1.20 lakh per acre) in market value (® 6.01 lakh per

As LA Act justifies addition of 30 per cent solatium and 12 per cent additional
amount on the value of land. Inclusion of 20 per cent enhancement, notional
rehabilitation cost and 10 per cent administrative cost was thus not justifiable. As
per the LA Act the admissible price of per acre land comes to % 8.53 lakh per
acre. Thus, there was an excess payment of € 1.29 crore’,

Similarly, land owners offered rates of € 30.80 lakh per acre for 28.79 acres and
% 30 lakh per acre for 7.475 acres of land. SJDA accepted the offered price as it
was less than the assessed the price of % 30.92 lakh per acre calculated by adding
30 per cent solatium (% 5.94 lakh per acre), 12 per cent additional amount
(® 2.37 lakh per acre) and 10 per cent administration cost (¥ 2.81 lakh per acre) on
the market value (= 19.80 lakh per acre). As per the LA Act, the admissible price
of land should be % 28.11 lakh per acre. Thus, there was excess payment of
₹ 91.58 lakh”.

Thus, there was an additional payment of हैं 2.21 crore. The CEO stated (July
2011) that while approving the assessment, the Board had taken into consideration
the additional funds that would have been expended on acquisition through LA
Collector. However, the fact remained that SIDA had made excess payment even
after allowing additional components of expenditures admissible under the Act.

2.2.8.3 Blockage of fund on acquisition of land in excess of requirement

To establish a Land Custom Station (LCS) at Fulbari, Jalpaiguri, SIDA initiated
(February 2010) land acquisition proposal for 10 acres with the LA Collector,
Jalpaiguri as per requirement of the Customs Authority, Gol, Siliguri, the primary
requiring body (RB). During the initial stage of the process of acquisition of the
land, RB intimated (April 2010) revised requirement of 6.51 acres. SJDA,
however, did not take cognizance of the revision and acquired 10 acres on
payment of हैं 1.93 crore (February 2010: 20.38 crore and August 2010:
₹ 1.55 crore) out of its own fund. Though SJDA raised (July 2010) a claim of
% 1.26 crore for 6.51 acres of land, the RB did not pay the amount (July 2011).
SJDA had also constructed a boundary wall around the entire area of 10 acres at
₹ 0.46 crore.

Thus, by acquiring land in excess of requirement, SJDA blocked % 0.83 crore
(land cost: हैं 0.67 crore and proportionate cost of boundary wall around the excess

2.2.8.4 Transfer of land in excess of actual acquisition

In accordance with the proposal (January 2004) of Central Reserve Police Force
Authority, Siliguri (CRPF) for suitable land for establishment of Group Centre
and Residential Colony, SJDA initiated land acquisition of 194.98 acres
(Darjeeling District: 105.72 acres and Jalpaiguri District: 89.26 acres) and paid
(July and December 2004) 7.16 crore to LA Collectors, Darjeeling and
Jalpaiguri. SIDA was handed over (August 2005) possession of 182.39 acres of
land. The balance 12.59 acres, being vested land was yet to be transferred (as of
July 2011). After completion of all formalities, SIDA transferred the possession
of the acquired land to CRPF in August 2005 and CRPF constructed boundary
wall around the transferred land as per demarcation (December 2008) done by
SJDA. However, on a joint survey conducted in April 2009, it was found that
actual land area was 203.56 acres. Thus, there was excess transfer of 8.58 acres
valuing हैं 0.60 crore'* free of cost to CRPF, depriving the State exchequer of
revenue.

CEO stated (July 201 1) that CRPF would be asked to hand over the excess land at
the earliest in order to avoid complications. But the fact remained that SIDA had
neither asked CRPF to hand over the excess land even after lapse of 30 months
from joint survey nor taken action to ascertain the reasons for such excess transfer
of land free of cost, indicating indifferent attitude.

2.2.8.5 Encroachment of land by unauthorised occupants

Out of 1207.958 acres of acquired land, SIDA failed to get physical possession of
48.748 acres of land acquired at हैं 7.83 crore, as these were encroached upon by
unauthorised occupants. Three such instances are indicated below:

a) SJDA did not get physical possession of 7.37 acres of land acquired
(December 2003) at Eastern Bye Pass-Don Bosco connector, as the land
had already been under encroachment at the time of acquisition. As a
result, infrastructure development at the site could not be initiated. Though
SJDA asked (April 2010) LA Collector, Jalpaiguri to refund हैं 1.16 crore
paid (September/ November 2003) for acquisition, the amount was yet to
be refunded (October 2011).

b) SJDA took on lease (February 2008) 3.118 acres of encroached land
(Nivedita Market: 1.442 acres and Hawkers Corner: 1.676 acres) from
Railways at a cost of % 4.87 crore! (paid between March 2006 and March

Reason for the same was not communicated, though called for. However, it was
noticed that SJDA had charged 20 per cent as incidental charges in a similar case
in April 2010.

e Delay in construction process after leasing

process should start within six months from the date of possession of land failing
which SJDA was at liberty to cancel the agreement and resume the land. It was,
however, seen that SJDA handed over 1.8 acres of land to Advanced Medical
Research Institute (AMRI) in November 2009 and three acres of land to Siliguri
Commerce College in June 2008. Though works have not been started in either of
these cases even after expiry of 20 to 36 months from allotment, action has not
been initiated by SJDA. It was observed that there was no system in place in
SJDA to monitor timely utilisation of the land allotted.

Public-Private Partnership (PPP). Of these, only one project (Amusement park at
Dagapur) has been completed as of March 2011, while remaining five were under
various stages of execution. Audit noted the following.

2.2.9.3 Allotment of land at Dagapur Amusement Park

In order to build, manage and maintain Amusement Park at Dagapur, Siliguri,

park.

₹ 0.93 crore over the period of 21 years. Thus, rejection of offer of RAPL resulted
in loss of F 0.45 crore”’.

While accepting the audit observation, CEO stated (July 2011) that initial project
report did not include the Multiplex. The fact, however, remained that the same
was accepted at a later stage which indicated inconsistent decision making on the
part of SIDA.

agitation, etc. Audit scrutiny revealed instances like intended benefits not being

SJDA awarded (July and August 2010) work orders for रैं 6.92 crore for
construction of three Sewage Treatment Plants (STP: I, II & गा)? at three
different places to three different agencies with the stipulation that the work

should be completed within four to six months from the date of issue of work

justifiable.

¢ Environmental clearance not obtained

Ministry of Environment and Forest, Gol notified (September 2006) a list of
projects or activities including construction of Common Effluent Treatment Plants
which required prior environmental clearance from the Pollution Controlling
authorities. Any construction without obtaining appropriate pollution clearance
certificate would therefore frustrate the ultimate purpose of MRCP.

Scrutiny of records revealed that SJDA, in violation of Gol’s instruction ibid, did
not obtain environmental clearance from the appropriate authority before
construction of the three Sewage Treatment Plants, indicating its indifference
towards environment as well as statutory obligations.

SJDA stated (November 2011) that necessary action would be taken for obtaining
required permission from proper authority as well as for early completion of the
STPs.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

2.2.10.3 Non-recovery of dues on execution of deposit works

SJDA executed construction works on behalf of various Government
Departments, Municipalities and other local bodies on ‘Deposit’ basis. The cost of
‘Deposit Works’ is required to be borne by the requiring bodies concerned. SIDA
is required to review the work monthly to see that no expenditure is incurred in
excess of the deposit obtained. If any excess is anticipated, expenditure on the
work should be restricted to the deposit received till acceptance of the additional
expenses of the party is obtained and additional funds are deposited.

Scrutiny revealed that an amount of & 12.13 crore was spent by the SJDA against
receipt of हैं 7.65 crore during 2006-11 in respect of 17 works as detailed in
Appendix 2.2.1. Expenditure of % 4.48 crore incurred in excess of the deposit was
not received from the requiring bodies by SJDA as of July 2011.

SJDA, while accepting the audit observation, intimated (July 2011) that
correspondence was on with the concerned departments for obtaining additional
funds as early as possible.

2.2.11 Internal Control

Internal Control is an integral component of an organisation’s management to
provide reasonable assurance that the operations are carried out economically,
effectively and efficiently to achieve the organisational objectives.

Audit noticed the following inadequacies in internal controls:

Budgetary controls in SIDA were deficient as indicated by delays in submission
of budget and significant deviation of actuals from the budget. Retention of
substantial funds in savings bank accounts depriving SJDA of interest income of
% 0.32 crore reflects lack of financial control. Absence of controls were evident as
SJDA failed to chalk out a repayment schedule although principal loan and
interest amounting to हैं 115.43 crore was overdue for repayment to GoWB.

Further, as per PW code, any construction work, estimates of which exceeds
र₹ 1.25 crore, requires sanction of Chief Engineer (CE). Test-check, however,
showed that out of 10 such works, technical approval of three were obtained from
CE (Construction Board), PWD, while the remaining seven were approved by SE,
SJDA. The Authority stated (July 2011) that the same had to be done as SE was
the highest technical authority under SJDA. In the exit conference it was
intimated that Government received proposal for sanction of post of CE in SJDA.
Principal Secretary, however, opined that such cases should have been referred to
outside agencies with appropriate technical expertise, like Engineering College
authorities.

70


Chapter-2-Performance Audit

The deficiencies in financial and operational management were indicative of
Departmental monitoring over functioning of SJDA, despite the Board of the
Authority being represented by Minister and Principal Secretary of the Urban
Development Department.

2.2.12 Conclusion

The objective of planning and development of SJDA was adversely affected as
SJDA, ignoring its statutory responsibility to prepare Land Use and Development
Control Plan within two years of notification, did not prepare the same in respect
of substantial part of its notified area, even after seven to 31 years of notifications.
As a result, development works undertaken by SJDA did not have the desired
linkage with long term development plan.

Budget management was flawed as would be evident from instances of delays of
29 months and 17 months in sending budget proposals to the Department.
Instances of non-initiation and abandonment of works due to non-receipt of
Budget allotments were also noticed.

Lack of internal controls over realisation of revenue led to loss on account of
unrealised toll charges, rents, etc. Retention of heavy cash balances in bank
savings and current accounts without gainfully investing them in term deposits led
to loss of possible additional interest.

Indifference towards financial interest of the Authority was also apparent from
instances of non-registration of acquisition of 462.43 acres of land, acquisition of
excess land than requisitioned by a requiring body, transfer of land in excess of
actual acquisition to a requiring body free of cost. Besides, 48.748 acres of land
acquired at हैं 7.83 crore was under encroachments

In the absence of comprehensive policy for fixation of prices for leasing out of
land, SJDA often took arbitrary decisions resulting in loss of revenue owing to
allotment of land at reduced rate, extending undue favour to lessees.

Execution of developmental projects in PPP model failed to yield the intended
benefits due to indifferent approach of the Authority in taking timely action.

The deficiencies in financial and operational management were indicative of
inadequate departmental monitoring over functioning of SJDA, though the
Government was represented in the board of the Authority by the concerned
Minister and Principal Secretary of the Urban Development Department.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

Recommendations

. SJDA should take immediate action for preparation of Land Use and
Development Control Plan for entire notified planning area in a
time-bound manner.

. SJDA should seriously pursue realisation of funds from

Chapter 3: Compliance Audit

3.1 Audit of non-compliance with Rules and Regulations

For sound financial administration and financial control it is essential that
expenditure conforms to financial rules, regulations and orders issued by the
competent authorities. This not only prevents irregularities, misappropriation
and frauds, but also helps in maintaining financial discipline.

In the course of audit of State Government Departments and their
functionaries, various cases of non-compliance with departmental codes and
manuals, Government orders/rules as well as non-adherence to the stipulations
imposed by various scheme guidelines etc. were noticed. Some major cases of
deviations from norms/rules are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs. As
these were arising only out of test-check of some offices, the Government
should ascertain occurrence of similar cases in other departments/districts and
evolve adequate mechanism to arrest these irregularities.

PUBLIC WORKS (ROADS) DEPARTMENT

3.1.1 _Unfruitful and avoidable expenditure

| Failure to adhere to technical norms in road construction and lacunae in
prior investigation resulted in unfruitful expenditure of रैं 2.13 crore and
avoidable expenditure of % 0.92 crore

Specifications for roads and bridges works of Indian Road Congress (IRC) and
Schedule of Rates of Public Works (Roads) Department! (PWRD) stipulate
that laying of Water Bound Macadam (WBM) over an existing bituminous
layer should be avoided since it may cause internal drainage of the pavement
at the interface of the two layers. However, in case laying of WBM is
absolutely necessary, it is desirable to pick out the existing bituminous layers
completely before laying of WBM. Furthermore, before undertaking any
strengthening work it is essential to conduct detailed investigations to find out
the profile of the existing crust and design the proposed crust thickness.

Scrutiny revealed (February 2011) that the Superintending Engineer, State
Highway Circle-VI awarded (July 2007) the work of strengthening of Barjora-
Maliara-Durlavpur Road (18.20 km stretch) to two agencies at the tendered
amount of % 5.29 crore for completion by November 2007. The scope of work
inter alia included laying of two layers of 75 mm WBM with stone metal
consolidation, 50 mm Bituminous Macadam (BM) as base course and 20 mm
mix seal surfacing as wearing course. The work was completed in April 2008
at a cost of % 6.11 crore. Departmental inspection revealed (June 2008) that
the road was severely damaged within two months of the completion of the
work i.e within the defect liability period of one year. The contractors initially
started (June 2008) repairing of the damages but later refused
(September 2008) to repair stating that the damages were due to failure of sub
grade and presence of rigid pavement underneath the WBM layers. An

1 Section 404.3.1 of IRC and clause B 7.3.1 of general specification of the Schedule of Rates, Public
Works (Roads) Directorate.

B


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

Enquiry Committee headed by the Chief Engineer, Planning and Quality
Assurance (the Committee) to investigate the premature damage of the road
work attributed (September 2009) it to the existence of underneath hard
bituminous layer of average thickness of 100 mm which were not taken into
consideration at the time of project preparation. As per the recommendations
of the Committee an estimate of % 8.59 crore was prepared (November 2009)
in phase-I for special repair of the entire road. The estimate proposed for
picking out the entire hard crust of 6.35 km (200 metre to 700 metre of each
kilometre of the entire stretch) of the severely damaged stretches of the road.
The estimate was yet to be approved (August 2011) by the Department.

Audit also observed that the Executive Engineer, Bankura Highway Division
did not carry out the required detailed investigations to find out the existing
crust composition and design the pavement accordingly before taking up of
the strengthening work. The WBM was laid without picking up the underneath
bituminous layer. Consequently, the entire stretch (18.20 km) of the newly
strengthened road surface got damaged. Pending approval of the said special
repair work, the division incurred an expenditure of % 0.92 crore on patch
repair of the entire stretch of the road between January and December 2010 to
make the road trafficable.

Thus, Department’s failure to adhere to IRC and PWRD specifications
regarding conduct of detailed investigation before strengthening work
rendered the expenditure of % 2.13 crore” on strengthening work unfruitful
besides avoidable expenditure of % 0.92 crore on repairs of 6.35 km stretch.
The Department stated (October 2011) that action was being taken on the basis
of recommendations of an Enquiry Committee constituted to investigate the
matter.

3.1.2 Avoidable expenditure in works under RIDF Scheme

Sera disregard of the RIDF guidelines two Departments incurred an

avoidable expenditure of % 1.47 crore on road repair within the defect
liability period, allowed price escalation of रैं 0.33 crore in a bridge work
and extended undue favour of रैं 0.43 crore to the contractors by allowing
longer road carriage and use of costlier bitumen.

National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development (NABARD) sanctions
loans under Rural Infrastructure Development Fund (RIDF) for improvement
of rural roads and bridges. Twenty seven divisions in the Public Works
Department (PWD) and Public Works (Roads) Department (PWRD)
responsible for maintenance of rural roads were audited during 2010-11 to
examine whether the works under RIDF loan were executed in accordance
with the prescribed guidelines. The results of audit are enumerated below:

3.1.2.1 Non-inclusion of performance guarantee clause in work agreement

Revised guidelines for schemes under RIDF issued (May 2002) by the Finance
Department, Government of West Bengal stipulated that performance

? %6.11 crore/18.20X 6.35

74



Chapter 3-Compliance Audit

guarantee for three years after completion of work should be obtained from the
agencies for the schemes worth हैं crore and above. Accordingly, the
Department was required to incorporate suitable clauses relating to the
performance guarantee in the contract agreement as a safeguard against the
defaulting contractors.

Superintending Engineer, Eastern Circle, PWD (SE/EC) awarded
(February 2006) the strengthening and shouldering work of Barasat- Basirhat
Road (26.40 km stretch) under RIDF-XI to a contractor at = 6.11 crore for
completion by August 2007. The work was completed in August 2007 at a
cost of % 5.28 crore. The terms and conditions of the contract included a defect
liability period of three years from the date of completion of the work. In case
of any defect detected during this period, the agency would be liable to rectify
the defect at its own cost. The contract, however, did not include any
performance guarantee clause for submission of bank guarantee by the
contractor for recovery of the cost towards repairing of defective works from
the defaulting contractor as envisaged in the RIDF guidelines.

Audit scrutiny revealed (June 2010) that the road was damaged within
March 2008 within the defect liability period. But, the agency did not take up
any repair work. Consequently, the Division incurred हैं 1.63 crore on patch
repair and special repair work to maintain the road trafficable between
April 2009 and February 2010 on the same stretch of the road.

The Department served (June 2010) a notice to the contractor to deposit the
cost of repair, but did not initiate further legal action. However, it forfeited the
earnest money of %0.12 crore and credited (February 2011) to the
Government account. The final bill valuing हैं 0.04 crore had been lying with
the division. The chances of recovery of the balance amount of & 1.47 crore
from the contractor remained bleak due to non-incorporation of any enabling
provision of “Performance Guarantee” in the tender agreement in violation of
the RIDF guidelines.

The Department stated (July 2011) that the Division was compelled to take up
the repair work due to non-trafficable condition of the road and it was not
possible to realise the cost from the agency due to hike in cost of bitumen. The
reply was not tenable as the Department failed to recover the cost of repair for
want of any performance guarantee.

3.1.2.2 Inclusion of cost-escalation clause beyond the ambit of RIDF
guidelines

Para 17 of revised guidelines (May 2002) of RIDF schemes stipulate that time
and cost over-run and the consequent cost escalation will not be permitted.

Superintending Engineer, State Highway Circle-IIl (SE/SHC-II), PWRD,
Siliguri awarded (November 2002) construction of bridge over Kalindri river
at Madiaghat under RIDF-VII to an agency at a cost of % 8.18 crore for
completion by May 2005. The work was completed in February 2006 at a cost
of % 8.46 crore including payment on cost escalation to the tune of
₹ 32.52 lakh.

Audit scrutiny (December 2010) revealed that even though the RIDF
guidelines prohibit payment of cost escalation, the SE/SHC-III included cost

75


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

escalation clause in respect of labour, material and POL in the Notice Inviting
Tender (NIT) issued in June 2002. The NIT also stipulated that admissibility
of such cost escalation was subject to the approval of the Government. The
SE, however, entered into (September 2002) an agreement with the contractor
including the clause of escalation without obtaining Government’s approval.
The Government accorded post-facto approval (February 2003) towards
acceptance of the tender subject to the condition that no payment due to
escalation would be allowed. However, the contractor on completion of the
work claimed (March 2006) escalation based on the relevant clause of the NIT
forming part of the contract and the Joint Secretary, PWRD_ paid
(November 2010) % 32.52 lakh to the agency on account of price escalation.

Thus, in violation of RIDF guidelines and the conditions of Government
sanction, SE/SHC-III had irregularly included escalation clause in the tender
document, which led to an avoidable expenditure of % 32.52 lakh on account
of undue payment of price escalation to the agency.

3.1.2.3 Undue favour to contractors by allowing longer road carriage and
use of costlier bitumen in the tender

Superintending Engineer, State Highway Circle-TV (SE/SHC-IV), Siliguri
PWRD awarded (April 2007) widening and strengthening work of two roads*
in North Bengal to two contractors at a total cost of हैं [13.95 crore for
completion by October 2008. The estimate was technically sanctioned by the
Chief Engineer, PWRD. The works were completed (April and June 2008) at a
total cost of € 13.95 crore.

Audit scrutiny revealed (December 2010) that the contracted rates of
Bituminous Macadam (BM) and Mix Seal Surfacing (MSS) in the two tenders
included longer road carriage for procuring packed bitumen of 60/70 grade
from the Indian Oil Corporation (IOC) depot at Uluberia which is located at
the southern part of the State. The SE had allowed carriage for a longer
distance (705 km and 677 km) from the Uluberia depot, although the same
was available at Siliguri depot (located in North Bengal) of Hindustan
Petroleum Corporation Limited (HPCL), which was only 52 km and 59 km
away from the work sites. Scrutiny of delivery challans of 735 MT bitumen
produced to Audit (out of 1109 MT of bitumen used in these two works),
however, revealed that the bitumen was purchased from the HPCL depot at
Siliguri. Delivery challans of the balance 374 MT of bitumen used in the work
was not produced to Audit. Further scrutiny of another reach (0 kmp to 6 kmp
and 8 kmp to 10 kmp of the GMS road), in the same road, revealed that during
the same period the Division considered carriage of bitumen from Siliguri
depot in the estimate.

Furthermore, the rates of the bituminous items in the estimates included cost
of carriage and 60/70 grade bitumen. However, as per Appendix-4 of IRC
guidelines on selection of the grade of bitumen, penetration grade 80/100
having lower viscosity should be used in high altitude. Moreover, use of

3 Peshoke- Mungpoo road (0 to 17 km) and Garidhutra- Mirik- Simanabusti (GMS) Road (from 30 to
45 km)

76


Chapter 3-Compliance Audit

80/100 bitumen is also cost effective’. Scrutiny of records also revealed that
200 MT of 80/100 bitumen was purchased and utilised in the work out of
available challan of 735 MT of bitumen supplied from the Siliguri depot.

In view of availability of the required bitumen at Siliguri Depot, the
Department was required to frame estimates considering road carriage from
Siliguri instead of longer carriage distance from Uluberia. Besides,
considering the suitability and economy, the Department could have
considered 80/100 grade bitumen instead of 60/70 grade. Thus, due to
imprudent decision of the CE to allow longer road carriage and consideration
of costlier grade bitumen in the tender estimate, the rates of BM and MSS
were inflated, which resulted in undue benefit of 743.28 lakh’ extended to the
contractors.

The Department stated (October 2011) that action was being taken on the basis
of recommendations of an Enquiry Committee constituted to investigate all the
three cases.

HOUSING DEPARTMENT

3.1.3 Avoidable expenditure in housing project

Lack of monitoring and non-adherence to the BIS specification in testing
of concrete by the West Bengal Housing Board led to avoidable payment
of € 1.87 crore as compensation to a contractor on a housing project.

Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) code stipulates that samples from fresh
concrete shall be collected, cured and be tested on 28 days in accordance with
1S:516-1959 for testing the strength of concrete. In all cases, the 28 days
comprehensive strength shall alone be the criterion for acceptance or rejection
of the concrete. Samples of at least three test specimens should be collected
for testing. Additional cubes may also be collected to determine the duration
of curing and checking the testing error.

West Bengal Housing Board (WBHB), an autonomous body under the
Housing Department entrusted with construction and outright sale of flats,
awarded (June and July 2005) construction of five HIG® buildings to an
agency through two separate tenders at a total cost of % 26.87 crore for
completion by March 2008. As of May 2011, the work was in progress and the
contractor was paid हैं 27.75 crore up to May 2011 including compensation of
% 1.26 crore. As per agreement the contractor was required to carry out the
required tests conforming to the BIS at his own cost. The Engineer-in- charge
(EIC) of the project was responsible for collection and testing of cubes as per
BIS code.

Audit scrutiny (October 2010) of the cube register maintained by EIC for
monitoring and supervision of the test of cubes revealed that mandatory tests
on concrete as per BIS code was not conducted by the agency in 26 out of

* As per schedule of rates of PWRD applicable to these works the rate of bitumen of 60/70 grade and
80/100 grade at Siliguri Depot were % 25062 and € 23114 per metric tonnes

°& 21.35 lakh on account of extra carriage and € 21.93 lakh for use 60/70 grade of bitumen.

° Three number of ground+ Underground+ eleven storied buildings and two number of ground+
Under-ground+ fifteen storied High Income Group (HIG) buildings of Eastern High at New Town,

77


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

129 columns in three towers. Further, when concrete in 14 columns (out of
102 columns for which mandatory test on concrete was conducted) in those
towers could not achieve the desired strength as per BIS specification on
28 days, rechecking of testing errors, if any, could not be conducted due to
inadequate collection of concrete samples. As a result, the work had to be
suspended in December 2005.

Consequently, the agency engaged (January 2006) two testing organisations’
to ascertain the quality of the concrete. As sufficient number of concrete cube
blocks was not preserved for retesting, non-destructive rebound hammer test
was conducted, which brought out contradictory and inconclusive results of
concrete strength. Meanwhile, with a view to examine the structures in
question and suggest remedial measures, the WBHB_ constituted
(February 2006) a Technical Committee headed by the Vice Chairman,
WBHB which recommended (May 2006) demolition of columns where either
cube crushing strength was not available (26 columns) or fell short of the
design strength (14 columns) on 28 days. The WBHB again entrusted
(September 2006) the Civil Engineering Department of the Indian Institute of
Technology, Khargapur to carry out the test on the structures in question to be
sure about the structural safety of the columns, which also could not give
(November 2006) any conclusive result.

Finally, the agency was instructed (April 2007) to resume work on those
Towers after dismantling the columns as suggested by the Technical
Committee. The columns were re-constructed by the contractor at their own
cost. Audit observed that due to lack of technical supervision and monitoring
on the part of EIC regular testing and collection of adequate samples of
concrete could not be ensured. As a result, the work remained suspended for
15 months and the WBHB had to pay हैं 1.87 crore® to the agency for rise in
price of building materials, compensation towards damages on tools and
plants, shuttering materials and cost of steel jacketing’. The suspension of
work by 15 months and consequent extra expenditure could have been avoided
had there been adequate monitoring for collection and testing of concrete as
per BIS code.

Thus, poor monitoring coupled with non-adherence to the BIS code resulted in
avoidable expenditure to the tune of € 1.87 crore.

3.1.5 Improper deployment of police personnel

In violation of Police Regulation of Bengal and Government orders to
take economic measures, the Superintendents of Police in North
24 Parganas and South 24 Parganas Districts unauthorisedly deployed
Home Guard and National Volunteer Force at the residences of retired
IPS officers for the services not related to security. Police cost of € 0.70
crore was also not recovered during December 2006 to June 2011.

Police Regulation of Bengal (PRB) lays down the rules for deployment of
police guards on permanent or temporary basis to Government organisation /
private parties. According to the rules, the Superintendent of Police (SP) may
at his discretion depute temporary police guards to private parties, provided
that, such guards should be in excess of the sanctioned allotment of the district
and proper sanction of Government for such deployment has been obtained.
To economise Government expenditure the Finance (Budget) Department
issued (June 2008) instructions against posting of Home Guard (HG)/National
Volunteer Force (NVF) in the residence of any officer, except where it is
specifically needed for security reasons and approval of Chief Secretary of the
State has been obtained. Further, in October 2009, Department of Civil
Defence decided in principle that no HG should be attached with any officer

"' Steel materials were issued departmentally at a recoverable rate of 28500 against the prevailing
market rate of %43000

79


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

after his superannuation. Accordingly necessary instructions were issued
(January 2011) to all district SPs for withdrawal of HGs attached to any
superannuated officer.

Test check (February and July 2011) of the records (December 2006 to
June 2011) of the SPs, North 24 Parganas and South 24 Parganas revealed that
police personnel/ HG/NVF were unauthorisedly deployed in the residences of
retired IPS officers/ higher officers in North 24 Parganas and South
24 Parganas Districts. However, no approval of the Chief Secretary was
obtained for such deployment. Besides, police cost was also not recovered
from them. The SPs in reply stated that officers of the rank of DIG and above
either in service or retired are entitled to have a security guard for protection
of his life. Scrutiny of disposition register, however, revealed that 37 HG/NVF
were deployed in the residences of retired IPS officers as Residential
Telephone Operator cum Messenger (RTCM)/ Official Telephone Operator
cum Messenger (OTCM) during December 2006 and June 2011. Deployment
of the HGs/NVFs as telephone operator and messenger for retired officers can
not be attributed to security reason. Police cost of & 0.70 crore’ in respect of
37 HG/NVFs for the period of their deployment was also not recovered from
those retired officers. Moreover, there were shortages in HG and NVF cadres
in two districts.

Consequent upon audit observations, SP, South 24 Parganas directed
(July 2011) the retired officers to release the NVF personnel attached with
them with immediate effect.

Thus, improper deployment of HG/NVF by the SPs of North 24 Parganas and
South 24 Parganas at the residence of retired IPS officers for reasons not
related to security led to unauthorised diversion of force from their primary
objective of maintaining law and order. Cost to the State exchequer, due to
non-realisation of police cost was हैं 0.70 crore.

The matter was referred to the Department in July 2011; reply is, however,
awaited (November 2011).

funds. The contention, however, is not tenable as Financial Rules do not
authorise such enhancement of price. Besides, delayed payment due to

Department. Two other DDOs"” did not furnish reply.

under Home (Police) Department and Health & Family Welfare Department in
violation of financial norms led to extra expenditure of 57 lakh.

(March 2011) all subordinate offices not to purchase articles at rates higher
than the MRP. Formal responses of the two Departments are, however,
awaited (November 2011).

३3.2. Audit against propriety/ expenditure without justification

Audit against propriety/ expenditure without justification endeavours to bring to
light every matter which appears to involve improper expenditure or waste of
public money or stores even though the accounts themselves may be in order and
no obvious irregularity has been committed. The objective is to support a
reasonably high standard of public financial morality and sound financial
administration and devotion to Government’s financial interests.

However, in many occasions instances came to notice where decision of the
Department or functionaries was questionable from the point of view of propriety.
In the succeeding paragraphs some major instances of Government expenditure
becoming either unfruitful or wasteful or were tantamount to undue benefit to
some outside agencies are discussed.

3.2.1 Procurement of unsuitable police vehicles for extremist affected areas

The Department procured two troop carriers worth = 91.73 lakh without
considering their suitability resulting in the vehicles not being used in
anti-terrorist operations for more than two years and consequent blockage of
funds,

M/s Ural India Limited (Company), a joint venture company'® of West Bengal
Industrial Development Corporation, is a heavy vehicle manufacturing company
at Haldia. On being approached by the Company, the Chief Secretary instructed
(February 2007) Director General & Inspector General of Police (Police
Directorate), Kolkata Municipal Corporation (KMC) and Fire and Emergency
Services Department to assess the suitability of vehicles for use in those
Departments. While both KMC and Fire Services Department opined
(August 2007) that the vehicle was unsuitable’? for their purpose, a troop carrier
vehicle was demonstrated (April 2007) by the Company in the police lines of
Superintendent of Police (SP), Paschim Medinipur.

In his reports (April and December 2007), the SP, Paschim Medinipur intimated
that the vehicle had no bomb blast proof technology. Moreover, the vehicle was
too big to be used along the narrow roads. It was also pointed out that field trials
of the vehicle could not be completed as the company had not agreed to keep the
vehicle with SP for seven days. The SP opined that the vehicle should be procured
only if found effective against land mine explosion, as the same was to be used in
extremist affected areas. Similarly, the Police Directorate expressed (March 2009)
reservation about the efficacy of the vehicle on account of high cost, inconclusive
field trials and non-availability of technical certificate regarding the vehicle being

" Between URALAZ of Russia, Motijug Group and WBIDC

to its large size.

SP, Purulia stated (May 2011) that the vehicle allotted to Purulia could not be
utilised in operation against extremists due to enormity of its size and consequent
inability to move along narrow roads in the hilly terrain. He further intimated that
the back of the vehicle being open, it was unsuitable for use in operations and was
being utilised for shifting of camps and movement of force necessitated during
major law and order problems in the district.

Thus, procurement of two unsuitable troop carrier vehicles worth = 91.73 lakh
ignoring the opinion of district police authorities, resulted in the vehicles not
being used for anti-terrorist operations for more than two years since their
procurement and blockage of funds.

The Department in its reply (July 2011) stated that no irregularities had been
found in procurement of MPVs, as these had been procured directly from
manufacturers. The reply, however, failed to justify purchase of vehicles unfit for
the avowed purpose.

3.2.2 Avoidable expenditure owing to overlapping of works

Superintending Engineer, State Highway Circle-VI undertook the
‘Improvement of Riding Quality of Panskura Durgachak Road’ immediately
before taking up of the Widening and Strengthening work on an overlapping
stretch to utilise the fund available under the Twelfth Finance Commission,
which resulted in avoidable expenditure of & 1.38 crore on bituminous items
of earlier work.

Superintending Engineer (SE), State Highway Circle (SHC)-VI, Medinipur
awarded (14 November 2008) the work of Improvement of Riding Quality”

20 Smooth riding capability of road by mending depressions, maintaining lines and grades and then covering
the road with wearing course.



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

(IRQ) on 10 kilometres (km) stretches (35 km to 41 km and 44 km to 48 km) of
Panskura Durgachak Road at a cost of %2.49 crore to four contractors for
completion by March 2009 under fund from the Twelfth Finance Commission
grant. The work inter alia mainly consisted of repairing of pot holes and laying of
bituminous courses like Bituminous Macadam (BM), Premixed Carpet and
Premixed Seal Coat on that stretch of the road. The work was completed by
February 2009 and the agencies were paid %2.6lcrore in January and
February 2009.

Scrutiny of records, however, revealed (February 2010) that the same SE awarded
(February 2009) the work of Widening and Strengthening (W&S) of 32.20 Km
(29.90 km to 62.10 km) of the same road to an agency at a cost of % 46.55 crore
for completion within 24 months. The estimate of the work was prepared in
April 2008 and the administrative approval was obtained (12 November 2008)
from Government of India under Central Roads Fund. The work inter alia mainly
consisted of Stone Metal Consolidation (SMC), laying of BM as base/binder
course and Semi Dense Bituminous Concrete (SDBC) or Mastic asphalt as
wearing course on the entire stretch of the road including 10 km stretches in
which IRQ was completed only in February 2009. The work was in progress
(November 2011) with an expenditure of € 16.43 crore.

Audit observed that there was no justification for awarding (14 November 2008)
the IRQ work on the overlapping stretches of the road even after obtaining the
administrative approval of W&S work (12 November 2008) which resulted in
superimposition on bituminous courses of the earlier work by the latter. If any
delay was apprehended for commencement of the W&S work, the pot holes and
depression could have been made good by executing routine repair and
maintenance works”'. This indicated that the Department did not have any
necessity or justification for taking up the IRQ work just before W&S work and
could have kept the road traffic worthy between November 2008 and
February 2009 by undertaking routine repair and maintenance works.

Scrutiny also revealed that the fund received (August 2008) by the division under
the Twelfth Finance Commission Grant for the IRQ work was to be utilised
within March 2009. To utilise the above fund within the financial year the
division hastily undertook the IRQ work.

Thus, deficiency in planning by the Department led to avoidable expenditure of
% 1.38 crore on bituminous courses of the earlier work (IRQ) which were being
superimposed by the latter work (W&S) on the same road stretches.

The Department stated (October 2011) that action was being taken on the basis of
recommendations of the enquiry committee constituted to investigate the matter.

PUBLIC HEALTH ENGINEERING DEPARTMENT

3.2.3 Infructuous expenditure in sinking of Rig Bored Tubewells

Four Public Health Engineering Divisions sunk rig bored tube wells in the
hard rocky parts of the State without ascertaining the availability of suitable
aquifer from the concerned authorities. This led to an infructuous
expenditure of 6.37 crore.

In the hard rocky parts of the Districts of Purulia, Bankura, Birbhum and western
part of Bardhaman, ground water is accumulated in restricted and limited’?
manner due to heterogeneity in aquifer system. The Public Health Engineering
Department (PHED) is entrusted with the function of supplying safe potable water
to the people living in that part by sinking of rig bored tube wells (RBTWs).
Though aquifers in these districts were rare and scarce”’, the PHED did not assess
beforehand the availability of aquifer at the sites selected by the Zilla Parishads
for successful sinking of RBTWs.

Audit scrutiny revealed that during 2004-11 the PHE Divisions sunk 13049
RBTWs in those four districts at the sites selected by the respective Zilla
Parishads without assessing the availability of potable ground water through the
State Water Investigation Directorate (SWID), the authority competent to assess
the quality and quantity of potable ground water in the State. Consequently,
sinking of 16407 RBTWs failed due to non-availability of suitable aquifer. These
also included 137 cases of repeat borings at the same selected sites although
suitable aquifer was not available earlier. As a result, the Department incurred an
expenditure of %6.37 crore on men and material on unsuccessful boring of
RBTWs.

All the four divisional authorities accepted (April-June 2011) that the incidence of
unsuccessful sinking of RBTWs could have been avoided had the sites been
selected after obtaining technical guidance and support from the SWID.

Thus, due to failure in obtaining technical assistance from SWID to ascertain the
availability of suitable aquifer before sinking of RBTWs, the Department had to
incur an infructuous expenditure of € 6.37 crore.

The matter was referred to the Government in June 2011 but reply has not yet
been received (November 2011).

Source: Information furnished by State Water Investigation Directorate

Source: Published materials of Central Ground Water Board, Government of India
