INDUSTRIAL RECONSTRUCTION DEPARTMENT

3.2.4 Loss due to imprudent release of loan

Industrial Reconstruction Department extended loan assistance to a sick
private industrial unit ignoring non-viability of its revival plan and absence
of any asset for collateral security. The unit was closed down in
November 2006. The Department took no action for recovery of its dues.
Resultantly, not only Government sustained loss of हैं 1.10 crore, but the basic
objective of revival of the unit did not fructify.

M/s Adhesive and Chemicals?” (A&C), a sick small scale industrial (SSI) unit
under private ownership, started suffering losses from 1989-90 onwards; the
accumulated loss increased to %5.37 crore, the net worth stood at minus
% 4.96 crore and total liabilities stood at हैं 5.47 crore in 2000-01. Value of fixed
assets (र₹ 0.44 crore) of A&C, which had already been mortgaged with Allahabad
Bank, was far below its total borrowings of हैं 4.81 crore as of March 2001. In
April 2001, A&C approached Industrial Reconstruction (IR) Department with a
revival scheme under ‘West Bengal Industrial Renewal Scheme, 2001’ (WBIRS).

Eligibility conditions for acceptance of any revival proposal under WBIRS
inter alia mandated the support of financial institutions / banks. It was also
stipulated that any soft loan granted by the State Government under the scheme
had to be appropriately secured.

Records of IR Department showed that while communicating its view on the
revival plan, Allahabad Bank had informed (September 2000) Government that
given the financial assistance of over हैं 2.60 crore already extended to A&C, it
would not be in favour of any further exposure to A&C. The Bank further
intimated (May 2001) that it would extend its help by way of re-schedulement of
its earlier loan subject to arrangement of long term finance by A&C. Thus, there
was no commitment from the Bank for releasing further loan to A&C. The cash
flow for seven years from the year 2001-02 included in the revival plan, however,
envisaged receipt of bank loans for sustaining business. The IR Department in
concurrence with the Finance Department sanctioned (October 2001) a soft loan
of हैं 1.10 crore to meet the gap of resources followed by another loan of
% 0.10 crore™* (March 2002) for liquidating the arrear sales tax dues of the unit as
per provisions of WBIRS. The principal amount of the loans was repayable in
eight years with a moratorium period of three years for the principal, while the
interest (8.75 per cent per annum) had no moratorium period. Though A&C had
no unencumbered assets to provide for reasonable collateral security, both the

27 Manufacturer and exporter of printing inks, reducers and thinners under the proprietorship of Ws.
Alirhoma Chemicals Private Limited.

अ The loan of €0.10 crore was not disbursed to A&C; rather it was directly adjusted in its commercial tax
account

Chapter 3-Compliance Audit

loans were stated to have been secured by a pari-passu charge of an adequate
amount of unencumbered assets ranking next to the charge in favour of Allahabad
Bank.

A&C, however, failed to achieve the performance level envisaged in the revival
plan owing to non-availability of need based working capital loan” from Bank
and it was finally closed down in November 2006. Nothing was on record to show
whether the Department had monitored the implementation of the revival plan.
Though no part of the Government loan was repaid till closure, the Department
took no legal action for recovery of its dues. Allahabad Bank, being the major
stake holder, however, filed (August 2007) a case in Debt Recovery Tribunal for
recovery of its dues (% 7.73 crore as of July 2007). Subsequently, the Government
became (August 2009) an added party to the recovery proceedings. The Bank and
A&C came to an out of the court settlement in May 2011, thereby leaving no
scope for realisation of the dues of the Government with interest thereon.

Thus, despite being aware of the fact that bank had not been interested in
providing loan assistance, essential for revival of A&C, and that the Government
loan would not be secured in absence of any encumbrance free asset of the unit,
Government released loan of % 1.10 crore. The Department did not take any
action for recovery of the loan given. This resulted in loss of Government money.

The Government stated (June and August 2011) that the loan had been sanctioned
considering the views expressed by the bank regarding viability of the scheme.
However, the reply was not acceptable as the bank expressed its inability to
extend further loan required as per the revival plan of the unit. Release of
Government loan was, therefore, against the tenets of financial prudence since
without securing bank loan, success of the revival plan was remote.

4}

Persistent/ Pervasive irregularities

An irregularity is considered persistent if it is of continuing nature and occurs
year after year. On the other hand, it becomes pervasive when it is prevailing in
the entire system. The scope of this section is to bring to light certain irregularities
of recurrent nature which have been noticed on several occasions during earlier
audits as well as in many departments. Recurrence of such irregularities is not
only indicative of lack of responsiveness of the Government, but also testifies
absence of effective monitoring. Such lack of seriousness on the part of the
Executive leads to deviations from the rules and regulations culminating in
weakening of the quality of administration.

GENERAL

3.3.1 Irregularities in management of cash by Drawing & Disbursing Officers

Non-adherence to the provisions of Treasury and Financial Rules by
23 07005 of six departments in nine districts resulted in financial
irregularities including unadjusted vouchers, unauthorised advances from
undisbursed cash, unexplained cash shortage, etc. amounting to हैं 2.69 crore
and retention of lapsed cheques amounting to हैं 79.83 lakh

As per West Bengal Treasury Rules (WBTR), no money is to be drawn from the
treasury unless it is required for immediate disbursement*’. All financial
transactions are to be recorded in the Cash Book of the respective units as soon as
they occur, under proper attestation by the Drawing and Disbursing Officer
(DDO). The cash book is required to be closed every day, while the head of the
office is required to physically verify the cash balance at the end of each month
and record a certificate to that effect. Bill-wise and date-wise analysis in respect
of closing balance is also to be recorded*!.

Scrutiny of the records of 23 DDOs of six departments in nine* districts
disclosed financial irregularities due to non-compliance with the above provisions
as shown in Appendix 3.2. Physical verification of cash conducted by these DDOs
at the instance of Audit during July 2010 to May 2011 revealed actual total cash
balance of % 42.60 crore on the day of verification, against the aggregate cash
book balance of % 45.29 crore, indicating a shortage of € 2.69 crore (Appendix
3.2). The shortages of cash was due to unadjusted vouchers (® 1.36 crore),
advances from undisbursed cash (¥ 1.07 crore) and unexplained shortage / theft
@ 0.26 crore).

temporary advances on account of travelling allowance, office expenses, VIP

(₹ 1.79 lakh) and RG Kar Medical College and Hospital (सं 4.55 lakh) who

counts out of undisbursed cash balance.

Further, physical verification of cash of the Accounts Officer, West Bengal
Secretariat, Finance (Accounts) Department revealed that 875 lapsed cheques
amounting to हैं 79.83 lakh, pertaining to various dates since 1976-77, were not
delivered and were retained as cash as on 04 May 2011. These lapsed cheques
reflected inflated expenditure, since, the amount had been drawn and shown as
expenditure in treasury under respective Heads of Account, whereas, the same
were still lying with the bank account of the DDO. Since these cheques had lost
their currency, steps need to be taken either to revalidate the lapsed cheques for
those which can be revalidated or attempts to be made to remit or refund the
amount to the respective Heads of Account.

Thus, non-adherence to the provisions of Treasury and Financial Rules and
inadequate internal controls over drawal and disbursement of cash by DDOs led

3.3.2 Lack of response of Government to audit

Principal Accountant General (Audit) (PAG) arranges to conduct periodical
inspection of Government Departments to test-check transactions and verify the
maintenance of important accounting and other records as per prescribed rules
and procedures. These inspections are followed up with Inspection Reports (IRs)
issued to the heads of offices inspected with copies to next higher authorities.
Important irregularities and other points detected during inspection, which are not
settled on the spot, find place in IRs.

Finance Department of Government of West Bengal, issued instructions
(June 1982) for prompt response by the executive to the IRs issued by the PAG to
ensure rectificatory action in compliance with the prescribed rules and procedures
and secure accountability for the deficiencies, lapses, etc. noticed during
inspection.

The heads of offices and next higher authorities are required to comply with
observations contained in the IRs and rectify defects and omissions promptly and
report compliance to PAG. Serious irregularities are also brought to the notice of
the Government by the office of the PAG. A six monthly report showing the
pendency of IRs is sent to the Principal Secretary/Secretary of the department to
facilitate monitoring settlement of outstanding audit observations in the pending
IRs.

Inspection Reports issued upto June 2011 relating to 428 offices in six
departments (Land and Land Reforms, Agriculture, School Education, Technical
Education and Training, Public Works and Housing Departments) disclosed that
1965 paragraphs relating to 611 IRs remained outstanding at the end of
June 2011. Of these, 75 IRs containing 84 paragraphs had been lying unsettled for
more than 10 years.

Department-wise and year-wise break-up of the outstanding IRs and Paragraphs
are detailed in Appendix 3.3.

These unsettled IRs contain 113 paragraphs involving serious irregularities like
theft/defalcation/misappropriation of Government money, loss of revenue and
shortage/losses not recovered/written off amounting to रैं 47.15 crore.
Department-wise and nature-wise analysis of the outstanding paragraphs of
serious nature showed the following position:

Audit committees, comprising of the Principal Secretary/Secretary of the
administrative department and representatives of the Finance Department and the
PAG (Audit), were formed in 507 out of 58 Departments of Government for
expeditious settlement of the outstanding Inspection Reports/paragraphs. Of the
50 Departments where audit committees were formed, meetings were held only
by sixteen Departments on 37 occasions from July 2010 to June 2011. As a result
of these meetings, 301 paragraphs were settled. Meetings were not held by the
other 34 Departments. Among the departments tabulated above, Public Works and
Housing Departments held three and one meetings respectively and settled six
paragraphs, while other four departments did not hold any meeting.

It is recommended that Government should ensure that a procedure is in place for
(i) action against the officials failing to send replies to IRs/paras as per the
prescribed time schedule, (ii) recovery of loss/outstanding advances/
overpayments in a time-bound manner and (iii) holding at least one meeting of
each audit committee every quarter.

the State Legislature, the State Government Departments are required to submit
suo motu replies to the audit observations relevant to them within one month.
Review of outstanding replies on paragraphs included in the C&AG’s Reports on
the Government of West Bengal upto 2009-2010 revealed that replies on
298 paragraphs (selected for discussion by PAC: 33 from 1997-1998 to
2009-2010 and not selected: 265 from 1981-1982 to 2009-2010) involving
40 Departments remained outstanding as of September 2011 as detailed in
Appendix 3.4.

3.4 Failure of oversight/governance

The Government has an obligation to improve the quality of life of the people.
Proper oversight on the part of Government would ensure that public money is
put to good use and the desired outcome of the same is derived.

The objective of this section is to bring to light cases of failure of oversight and
governance at various levels of administration. Resultantly, funds released by
Government for creation of assets for benefit of public remained
unutilised / blocked or expenditure incurred thereon became unfruitful/
unproductive/infructuous.

Some major cases of laxity in governance resulting in avoidable/ unfruitful/
additional expenditure from the Government exchequer are discussed in this
section.

HEALTH AND FAMILY WELFARE DEPARTMENT

3.4.1 Wasteful expenditure on revival of a hospital

The objective of reviving the Mayo Hospital, Kolkata did not fructify owing
to inaction and indifference of the Health and Family Welfare Department.
Resultantly, Government had to shoulder wasteful/unfruitful expenditure of
= 10.25 crore for meeting liabilities of the joint venture company.

With a view to revive Mayo Hospital”, Kolkata, Government decided (July 1994)
to form a joint venture company namely, Eastern India Institute of Medical
Sciences and Research Hospitals Limited (EITMS). An agreement to that effect
was executed (June 1994) between Government and Emergency Doctors Service
Private Limited (EDS), promoter of the Joint Venture Company. It was agreed
that the Government, the Promoter and the Public would have equity
shareholding of 26 per cent, 25 per cent and 49 per cent respectively. In the event
of the project not materializing, EDS was to bear all pre-operative expenses“.

The Department handed over (November 1994) land and buildings of the hospital
to EIIMS on lease for a period of 30 years with the right to mortgage the same to
any Financial Institution. It was agreed (November 1994) that the EIIMS would
pay a premium of % 1.52 crore in the form of 1,52,400 equity shares of
= 100 each. The EIIMS was to pay lease rent of हैं 10 lakh per annum starting
from the first week of January 1995. Through a subsequent rectification deed
(October 1999) signed by Joint Secretary, the Department agreed to convert the

” The hospital, situated at Strand Road, Kolkata was taken over by Government in August 1982.
The Department could not furnish the total cost of revival project.

* Emergency Doctors Private Ltd, Private Health Plan (P) Lid and Others

Not estimated at the time of execution of the agreement.



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

total lease rent payable (€ 3.048 crore less हैं 5.27 lakh already paid) over thirty
years into equity shares of the company deliverable to the Government. This
resulted in 65 per cent share being transferred to State Government, as against
26 per cent initially agreed upon making the joint venture company a Government
Company. The reasons for converting the lease rent as equity share have not been
stated to Audit.

For construction of the outpatient department (OPD) of the hospital, EIIMS
acquired a loan’ of %5.34crore from HUDCO between January and
October 1999 by mortgaging the property of the hospital. It was observed that
EIIMS defaulted in repayment and HUDCO moved (September 2003) the Debt
Recovery Tribunal for realisation of outstanding sum of 6.39 crore’’. The
realisable amount increased to % 17.10 crore as per demand (May 2009) of
HUDCO, against which the Government paid (May 2009) ₹ 8.60 crore in an out
of court settlement to get back the mortgaged property from HUDCO.

The EIIMS engaged (February 1999) an agency ‘A’*” for construction of OPD
and diagnostic unit for which it was liable to pay % 1.48 crore*® (as of
February 2002). The agency filed (2003) a winding up petition in the High Court
for recovery of its outstanding dues against EIIMS. As per High Court’s order,
Government paid (June 2009) ₹ 1.65 crore to the agency ‘A’.

The Department ultimately cancelled the lease with EIIMS in June 2010 through a
gazette notification and transferred back the land and building of the Mayo
Hospital to the Department, which was subsequently handed (October 2010) over
to Kolkata Medical College & Hospital for serving as its second campus.

Audit scrutiny (January 2011) of the records of the Director of Health Services
revealed that the Department did not recover any amount from the promoter,
though as per the agreement, the promoter (EDS) was to bear all pre-operative
expenses in case the project did not materialise. Department also expressed
(April 2011) ignorance about the balance funds lying with the EIIMS out of the
loan funds or capital invested by it as well as about physical progress of work
made by EIIMS in revival of the hospital. This was indicative of negligent
approach of the Government, considering the fact that Government had equal
representation in the Board of Directors of EIIMS and was in possession of
65 per cent of shares.

Thus, Government was forced to bear wasteful expenditure of हैं 8.60 crore
towards repayment of HUDCO loan, while हैं 1.65 crore paid (June 2009) to the
contractor remained unfruitful, leaving the promoter (EDS) unaccountable. The
basic objective of reviving the Mayo Hospital did not fructify.

(October 1999), which led to accrual of additional shares to Government.
Evidently, the Department due to inaction and failure to monitor the activities of
the joint venture company and invoke the clause in the agreement with the
promoter to bear all pre-operative expenses in case of the project not
materialising, compromised the interest of the Government.

3.4.2__Non-functioning of newly constructed health centres

Newly created health services infrastructure and medical equipments/
furniture worth हैं 2.05 crore under Basic Health Project remained unused for
two to five years due to non-posting of additional manpower and locational
disadvantages. Resultantly, the objective of improvement of health services
remained unachieved, besides possibility of damage of the infrastructure due
to non-use.

With a view to improve the health conditions in the State through quantitative and
qualitative improvement of health services, Health & Family Welfare (H&FW)
Department had taken up (May 2001) implementation of KFW® Funded Basic
Health Project. Eight districts” were selected*! for implementation of the scheme
at a total cost of ¥ 214 crore. The components of the project inter alia included
civil works, including construction of additional buildings, for upgradation of
health facilities and supply of equipment and furniture to the upgraded facilities.
The Project was managed and implemented by the Programme Director and
Ex-Officio Special Secretary, H&FW Department. Under phase 1°? of the project,
total 215 works (upgradation of 38 Block Primary Health Centres, 91 Primary
Health Centres and 86 Sub-Centres) were completed between August 2005 and
November 2008 at a total cost of € 60.44 crore.

DEPARTMENTS

3.4.3 Wasteful expenditure on construction of Kolkata Kala Kendra

Construction of Kolkata Kala Kendra remained at the foundation level even
after lapse of 11 years from its initiation. Repeated change in scope of work
coupled with failure of the Department to expedite the progress of work not
only led to steep enhancement in estimated cost, but rendered foundation
piling work worth & 1.01 crore wasteful.

Information and Cultural Affairs (I&CA) Department, on the occasion of
tercentenary of Kolkata, entered into an agreement (January 2003) with Agency

97


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

“A”® for construction of a cultural complex named Kolkata Kala Kendra
(KKK) at Rowdon Square, which had a water body. The work aimed at
environmental preservation and enrichment and beautification of the water body.
The Public Works (PW) Department was to supervise the construction work. The
agency placed (June 2004) an estimate of हैं 15.87 crore®' for making KKK of
international standards, particularly in respect of stage with hydraulic lifting
system, back stage, digital lights and sound system along with most modern
greenroom facilities and soundless AC system. However, detailed drawings and
specifications were not submitted by the agency to Government. The Secretary,
KKK intimated (June 2011) that as the proposal had been initiated by the
Principal Secretary, I&CA Department and endorsed by the Chief Engineer,
Public Works (PW) Department, Detailed Project Report and Working Drawings
were not prepared. The Secretary, KKK issued (November 2004) work order to
Agency “A” for plinth level work™. As the whole complex was to rest on pile
foundation, Agency “A” constructed (November 2004 to — June 2006)
267 Reinforced Cement Concrete Bored piles.

Audit Scrutiny (May 2011) of the records of I&CA Department revealed that
Department of Civil Engineering, Jadavpur University (JU) assessed (June 2006)
the quality of pile-foundation and found that about 36 per cent of the piles were
defective either in the form of deformed shafts or short in effective length,
affecting the actual pile capacity vis-d-vis designed capacity. Inspection by JU
further revealed that defective pile construction was mainly attributable to
deficient examination of sub-soil condition in the pool area by the Agency and
inappropriate technique adopted for piling; and laxity in quality control.

Based on remedial measures suggested (June 2006) by JU, Agency “A”
constructed 75 additional piles and handed over (June 2008) the site to I&CA
Department. Agency “A” was paid % 2.71 crore up to November 2008 for the
work, as per valuation made by PWD. No payments were made for construction
of the 75 additional piles.

Present condition of the site for Kolkata Kala Kendra

Chapter 3-Compliance Audit

In June 2008, PWD was entrusted with construction of the remaining work of
KKK, which prepared (December 2008) an estimate of € 42.03 crore. The reasons
cited (December 2008) for the increase in the estimated amount were escalation in
price of basic material as well as incorporation of all possible items of work
intended to build KKK as a modern cultural complex of state-of-the-art nature.
I&CA Department accorded (September 2009) administrative approval for
र₹ 42.03 crore. As of March 2011, piling work was completed by PWD, while
process of tendering for construction of superstructure was in progress. Executive
Engineer (EE), PWD, West Kolkata Division intimated (September 2011) that
only a part of the old foundation work worth हैं 1.70 crore executed by Agency
‘A’ could be made use of. EE further stated (November 2011) that this was due to
change in foundation design on account of revised architectural drawing.

Thus, not only work worth %1.01 crore® executed by Agency “A” became
wasteful, but construction of the Kolkata Kala Kendra remained at the foundation
level after lapse of 11 years from initiation. I&CA Department failed to expedite
progress of the work, which was further compounded by subsequent change in the
scope of work. Supervision by PW Department of the quality of work executed by
the agency was inadequate.

The Joint Secretary, I&CA Department while admitting the wasteful expenditure,
stated (September 2011) that the project could not materialise due to procedural
formalities. He further added that delay in getting clearances from various
authorities, frequent alteration of original plan and other consequential issues by
different technical bodies were the major reasons for delay.

PASCHIMANCHAL UNNAYAN AFFAIRS DEPARTME

3.4.4 Unfruitful expenditure on employment generation scheme

Soe aaa goat rearing scheme introduced by Paschimanchal Unnayan Parshad

(PUP) in Birbhum and Purulia Districts did not succeed, as the PUP, District
Magistrates and Zilla Parishads had not ensured supply of goats by a private
agency. Resultantly, expenditure of & 2.06 crore on construction of 752
unused sheds remained unfruitful.

With the aim of generating self-employment among poor rural women in
Birbhum and Purulia Districts through introduction of goat rearing scheme,
Paschimanchal Unnayan Parshad (PUP) under the Paschimanchal Unnayan
Affairs Department entered into separate agreements™ with a private agency
(Ascon Agro products exporter and Builders Private Limited) in November 2006
(Birbhum) and June 2007 (Purulia). Under the scheme, Self Help Groups (SHG)*
were to be created, who were to breed and rear Black Bengal Goats. The scheme

° 2.71 crore minus @ 1.70 crore
“ Signed by respective Sabhadhipati, Zilla Parishad and District Magistrate on behalf of PUP.
°° SHGs were created under the scheme Swarnajayanti Gram Swarojgar Yojana.

envisaged round the year supply of marketable goats to a mutton and chevon
production unit proposed to be set up in the State by the agency.

The scheme further envisaged that Zilla Parishad (ZP) would be responsible
inter alia for arrangement of covered area for housing the goats and proper co-
ordination and care in the project. They were also responsible for arrangement of
subsidy for SHGs through District Rural Development Cell (DRDC) for
infrastructure support and providing cooperation in implementation of the project.

According to the agreements executed between the agency and ZP/PUP, the
agency was to provide breedable Black Bengal Goats to SHGs for the project
period of five years and help them in development of superior breeding stock,
provide medicines, vaccines, veterinary services and insurance for breeding stock
and the kids. The agreements with the agency were, however, silent about
schedule of supply of goats to co-ordinate with the construction of goat sheds.
There was no penal provision in case of non-supply of goats by the agency.

Audit scrutiny (March and June 2011) of the records of District Magistrates

= 2.23 crore to the DMs, who in turn released the amounts to the PSs during
2006-07 for construction of 848 sheds. As of March 2011, 755 sheds®” were
constructed at a cost of हैं 2.12 crore. It was noticed that goat rearing had started
only at three sheds” from 2009-10, while 752 completed sheds remained unused
owing to non-supply of goats by the agency. The district authorities, PUP or the
ZPs did not take up the matter with the agency to ensure supply of goats as per
agreements or to ascertain reasons for non-supply. The objective of generation of
self employment was, thus, not achieved as of June 2011 despite incurring an
expenditure of € 2.06 crore” for construction of sheds.

-CONVENTIONAL ENERGY
DEPARTMENT

| POWER AND RCES

3.4.5 Implementation of hydel power projects in Darjeeling

Even after lapse of seven to eight years from approval, all six hydel power
projects in Darjeeling District remained either non-starters or defunct owing
to failure of WBREDA to address primary ground level issues. Resultantly,
% 1.25 crore spent on these projects was rendered unfruitful/wasteful; while
₹ 1.23 crore remained parked in WBREDA’s current bank account for three
to eight years.

West Bengal Renewable Energy Development Agency (WBREDA), an
organisation under the Power and Non-conventional Energy Sources (P&NES)
Department of Government of West Bengal, is the nodal agency inter alia
responsible for setting up small power projects using alternative sources of
energy. Proposals for implementation of such projects are initiated by the
WBREDA. After the same is approved techno-commercially by the P&NES
Department, financial sanction is accorded by the Ministry of Non-conventional
Renewable Energy (MNRE) ™ Government of India. It is the responsibility of
WBREDA to assess availability of encumbrance-free land and viability of the
project and identifying beneficiary committees before initiating the draft
proposals.

For providing electricity in remote villages of Darjeeling District, MNRE
sanctioned’! (between March 2003 and August 2004) six hydel power projects

20 Karlier known as Ministry of Non-conventional Energy Sources

Khola Micro Hydel Power Project 50 kw (2004-05), Reaing Khola Micro Hydel Power Project 50 kw

State Government (GoWB). For implementing these six projects, WBREDA

and August 2004) and GoWB (&1.45crore during February 2004 to
March 2008).

Scrutiny (June 2011) of the records of WBREDA in respect of implementation of
these projects revealed that three of these projects remained non-starter, one was
dropped after partial implementation, while another, though commissioned, failed
to deliver the intended benefits and was ultimately closed down. Out of
₹ 2.48 crore released by Gol and GoWB, € 1.25 crore was spent on three of these
projects during April 2005 to September 2009, while हैं 1.23 crore was parked in

WBREDA ultimately dropped (June 2010) all small, mini and micro hydel power
projects attributing the decision to law and order problems in Darjeeling District.
Scrutiny of records of WBREDA, however, revealed various instances of

Subsidy was restricted to cost of electro mechanical equipment and 50 per cent cost of civil works.

102


Chapter 3-Compliance Audit

deficiencies in ground level planning and preparedness which had culminated in
stoppage of the projects midway or project not taking off at all, as discussed in
subsequent paragraphs.

i) Pachengkhola Micro Hydel power project

WBREDA started (August 2005) the work at Pachengkhola through a private
contractor A® at a project cost of € 55.95 lakh. However, a private tea company
raised objection in October 2005 on the ground that the construction site was on
the land belonging to the tea company and the company had one defunct hydel
power project awaiting overhauling in that area. WBREDA decided (June 2006)
to shift the project to Rebongkhola with same technical specifications and rate.
The same contractor ‘A’ conducted a pre-survey (July 2006) at Rebongkhola and
took up the work (June 2007). Subsequently, Contractor A stated that the project
site at Rebangkhola was different in character” and as such, estimated cost should
be revised. WBREDA revised the project cost to % 63.58 lakh and issued work
order in June 2007. The Project cost was further revised to % 105.25 lakh
(July 2009) taking into account additional cost for purchase of material through
Sikkim. The contractor executed part of civil work and was paid % 43.11 lakh”
during August 2005 to September 2009.

However, in consideration of factors viz. cost escalation, locational disadvantages,
demand from villagers for grid power and inability to form beneficiary
committee, rising number of beneficiaries etc. WBREDA decided (June 2010) to
drop the Rebongkhola hydel power project.

Thus, improper selection of site and lack of groundwork namely, identification of
encumbrance free land, formation of beneficiary committee, assessment of cost
escalation of the project at the relocated site, resulted in wasteful expenditure of
₹ 43.11 Lakh.

ii) Daraykhola Micro Hydel power project

The work order of the Daraykhola project was issued (March 2005) to a
contractor A at an estimated cost of हैं 55.65 lakh, which was revised
(September 2005) to हैं 69.82 lakh. The work started in March 2005 concluded in
July 2006 and the plant was inaugurated in September 2006. The contractor was
paid (between April 2005 and October 2008) ₹ 74.87 lakh. Though Project Report
envisaged supply of 140 watt electricity to 300 households for eight hours per
day, WBREDA stated that a commitment for un-interrupted power supply under
favourable conditions had been made, subject to proper operations and
maintenance. However, the plant could supply power for three to four hours in a
day and WBREDA attributed the same to failure of the beneficiary committee.

3 M/s AN Sharma & Co.

™ The site of Rabengkhola could be approached only from Sikkim side and all material purchased attracted
additional taxes and duties. Besides there was additional cost for carriage of construction materials by head
load. These resulted in increase in cost of Civil Works. Besides, Construction of road was essential for

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

The beneficiary committee demanded (August 2007) 24 hours power supply
through grid power from WBSEDCL” or Remote Electricity Development
Corporation/ Darjeeling Gorkha Hill Council, indicating lack of agreement and
co-ordination with the beneficiary committee. Though WBREDA claimed that the
project was capable of generating electricity for 24 hours a day, the beneficiary
committee closed down the power house (September 2007).

Thus, the project failed in delivering its objectives as it could not meet the
expectations of the beneficiaries.

(iii) | Naxal Small Hydel Power project

WBREDA handed over the work of execution of the Naxal project on turnkey
basis (May 2003) to West Bengal State Electricity Board (later renamed as
WBSEDCL), which awarded the works to contractors during December 2004 to
June 2007. Scrutiny revealed that no work, apart from pre-project survey (cost:
₹ 1.30 lakh) could be started, as the site was in the midst of various plantations”,
residential huts, burial ground etc. WBSEDCL and WBREDA decided
(July 2008) to terminate the contracts. WBREDA on field inspection
(November 2009) observed that the areas remained under private occupation
despite release of 6.10 lakh (February 2008) to the DM, Darjeeling for payment
of compensation to the Project affected persons. Moreover, it was also observed
that water in the river where the project was to be constructed was available only
for four to five months in a year.

WBREDA resolved (March 2009) to appoint West Bengal Green Energy
Development Corporation Limited for supervision and execution of the work.
However, no development in the matter was recorded as of October 2011.

WBREDA admitted (June 2011) that hydrology and meteorology of the site had
changed and fresh study would be required. In these circumstances the prospect of
implementation of this project seemed bleak.

While accepting the facts (June 2011), WBREDA attributed non-execution of the
projects to law and order problems in the Darjeeling. The reply, however, did not
address the issues of deficiencies in ground level planning and preparedness on its
part to implement the projects.

Thus, after lapse of seven to eight years from approval, none of these six hydel
projects in Darjeeling have fructified. This was mainly attributable to failure of
WBREDA to address primary issues like, identification of encumbrance free land,
assessment of viability of projects, formation of beneficiary committee and taking
into account their expectations from the projects etc. Resultantly, expenditure of
₹ 1.25 crore either became wasteful or unfruitful, while Government funds of
₹ 1.23 crore remained parked in WBREDA’s current bank account for three to
eight years.

” West Bengal State Electricity Distribution Company Limited
” Plamation of rubber, betel nut, cardamom, etc.

104


Chapter 3-Compliance Audit

In a reply endorsed by the Department, WBREDA stated (November 201 1) that it
had social obligation to implement renewable energy projects as technology
demonstration projects and that operation and maintenance of these projects did
not come under its purview of work. It was further stated that the local
administration was responsible for development of adequate infrastructure
facilities.

As regards Pachengkhola (shifted to Rebangkhola) project, it was intimated that
being encouraged by the effort of one individual, who had been generating
electricity for local people by tapping water from a nearby stream, WBREDA had.
undertaken the project. His dissociation from the effort, compounded by lack of
infrastructural development and erratic availability of water at the stream, led to
failure of the project.

Regarding Daraykhola project, WBREDA attributed the failure to
non-co-operation from the local bodies.

The reply, however, indicates lack of careful assessment of infrastructural
deficiencies in concerned areas by WBREDA. Further, failure of a project due to
dissociation of a certain individual indicates that the project had been taken up
without proper viability study and finalisation of modalities of operation and
maintenance.

INDUSTRIES AND HORTICULTURE DEPARTMENTS

3.4.6 Infructuous and idle expenditure under ASIDE scheme

Objective of creation of export auction infrastructure for a flower market
was not achieved owing to failure of a Government Corporation and Food
Processing Industries and Horticulture Department in ensuring temporary
relocation of flower vendors. This resulted in idling of expenditure of
= 1.84 crore, while expenditure of 0.78 crore became infructuous.

A project for setting up an export auction centre with modern infrastructure
facilities at Mallickghat Flower Market, Kolkata was taken up (March 2006) by
the State Level Export Promotion Committee (SLEPC)” at a total cost of
% 25.43 crore” under the scheme of Assistance to States for Developing Export
Infrastructure and Allied activities (ASIDE)*’, for completion in 48 months.
Ministry of Commerce and Industry, Government of India accorded approval of
Central assistance of हैं 10 crore and released (March 2006) first instalment of
5 crore to West Bengal State Food Processing and Horticulture Development

5 Chaired by the Chief Secretary and Director of Industries being the Convener

” Revised to €20 crore, of which Central assistance was € 10 crore

* ASIDE was launched by Government of India in March 2002 for creating appropriate infrastructure for
development and growth of exports. Director of Industries (DI), Government of West Bengal was the nodal
officer for implementation of ASIDE projects in the State. Assistance under ASIDE project is fully borne by
the Central Government and released for implementation of Export oriented projects. Administrative
expenses under such works are not to be incurred out of Gol funds and to be borne by the State Government.

105


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

Corporation Limited (Corporation)*', the implementing agency. The Director of
Industries (DI) under the Commerce and Industries Department, the nodal officer
for implementation of ASIDE projects, released (July 2006) € 1 crore to the
Corporation as State component. The project envisaged construction of 284 shops,
sitting arrangement for 2000 to 3000 flower vendors, cold storage, sorting,
grading, auction hall, conference hall, lodging facilities for auctioneers, bank,
administrative office, basement parking area, etc.

Availability of land was one of the criteria for taking up a project under ASIDE.
The land for Mullickghat flower market, which originally belonged to Kolkata
Port Trust (KPT), was transferred in favour of Government of West Bengal in
June 2002. In order to facilitate construction of infrastructural facilities,
temporary relocation of flower vendors was necessary, for which another KPT-
owned space at Jagannathghat was identified. The Food Processing Industries and
Horticulture Department paid (June 2010) ₹ 1.22 crore®? (₹ 78.37 lakh as licence
fees, municipal and other taxes for 22 months from April 2008 to August 2010
and हैं 43.48 lakh as security deposit) to KPT through the Corporation for the land,
for temporary relocation of flower vendors.

Out of % 6 crore, the Corporation spent % 1.84 crore between August 2006 and
June 2010 on the project towards fees for sanctions and approvals from statutory
authorities® (₹ 113.15 lakh), project management consultancy fees (% 29.40 lakh),
construction of temporary infrastructure for relocation of flower vendors
(© 38.18 lakh) and miscellaneous/administrative expenses (रैं 3.21 lakh). However,
utilisation certificate for the entire Central assistance of = 5 crore was furnished in
September 2007.

Audit scrutiny (January 2011) of the records of the Director of Industries revealed
that poor response among the vendors of the Mallickghat flower market for
shifting to a temporary relocation was a major impediment in execution of the
project. Moreover, it took more than two and half years for the Corporation to
obtain clearances for the Project from different authorities including KPT. In view
of slow progress of implementation, Gol cancelled the project in July 2009 and
demanded (June and August 2009) refund of the entire Gol assistance of € 5 crore
with interest® at the rate of 10 per cent from the date of release of fund. The

November 2011. The State contribution of हैं crore was retained by the
Corporation as of August 2011.

lack of initiative, coordinated effort and ineffective monitoring on the part of the
FPI&H Department to provide a favourable condition for implementation of the
project coupled with delay in obtaining clearances from the appropriate

URBAN DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT

3.4.7 Delay in construction of a bus stand

Even after lapse of 10 years from its initiation, a bus stand at a popular
tourist destination (Digha) remained a non-starter resulting in idle
expenditure of हैं 1.08 crore, besides frustrating the intended benefits to
commuters concerned.

Digha Sankarpur Development Authority®’ (Authority)
under Urban Development (UD) Department decided
(October 2000) to construct a Central Bus Stand at Digha
to reduce traffic congestion and parking problem in the
lone vehicular road of Digha and thereby arrest road
accidents. The land identified for construction belonged to
Digha Development Scheme (DDS) under Development
and Planning (D&P) Department. The Authority had
neither got the title of the land transferred in its name, nor
obtained necessary permission from the D&P Department
Incomplete Central Bus Stand at Digha (October 2011) before commencement of the work. The Authority started
the work in July 2001 at an estimated cost of हैं 35.54 lakh
through an agency “A” with the target of completion by
January 2002. Subsequently, the Authority enhanced
(March 2003) the scope of work*’” and signed
(November 2004) a Memorandum of Understanding
(MoU) with the same agency “A” for construction of the
bus stand within 24 months in Public Private Partnership
model at an estimated cost of € 1.90 crore.

Incomplete terminus building (October 2011)

४४ Licence fee and other taxes paid up to February 2010

Known as Digha Development Authority prior to September 2003
* Incorporating therein construction of boundary wall, stalls, bus-bay, bus terminal building, rest room, bathing units,
lavatory complex, convenience shops, etc.

107


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

However, considering non-transfer of title of the construction site from D&P

stopped. Before stoppage of work, the Agency had completed works** worth
₹ 60.09 lakh (as of May 2005) against which full payment was released by
March 2008.

Subsequently, the UD Department notified (December 2006) the merger of DDS
with Authority transferring all its assets and liabilities to the latter. The work of
construction of the Bus Stand was re-started in April 2007. As of December 2010,
eight works® of construction for € 1.36 crore were executed in piecemeal manner
against which & 1.08 crore” was paid (including = 60.09 lakh paid earlier) up to

intimated (July 2011) that absence of any permanent EO and non-holding of any

bus stand was formally inaugurated in November 2010 for use.

Scrutiny of the records of the Authority along with joint physical verification of
the site, however, revealed (June 2011) that in spite of formal inauguration, the
bus stand remained unused with construction works of south boundary wall,
balance works for 36 stalls, finishing work of the first floor of terminal building
and remaining part of concrete pavement were yet to be completed. Due to non-
commencement of operations of the bus stand, the objective of solving the acute
parking problem and minimising traffic congestion in the lone vehicular road in
Digha was not achieved. The local police authority expressed concern (May 2011)
over increased traffic congestion in Digha, which is supported by continuing trend
in occurrence of accidents. The Authority in its reply (October 2011) stated that
the bus operators were unwilling to use the bus stand for non-construction of bus
bays, ete and non-introduction of one way traffic movement through a bye pass
road (Northern bye- pass), which would provide inward traffic to the bus stand.
The Authority also acknowledged the requirement of widening and strengthening
of the bye-pass road, before putting the bus stand in use.

Thus, after 10 years from initiation of construction work and more than eight
years from finalisation of composite plan, the Central Bus Stand at Digha, a
popular tourist destination, remained a non-starter with idle investment of
₹ 1.08 crore. Resultantly, traffic congestion in Digha remains a matter of concern
as continuing trend of accidents constitutes definite risk to human life.

The matter was referred to Government in July 2011; reply had not been received
(November 2011).

3.4.8 Undue financial aid to contractor

The main objective of the PPP project remained unachieved. Besides, KMDA
could not avail the competitive rate due to change in land use pattern after
acceptance of bid and also extended undue aid of हैं 2.57 crore by under
valuing the rate of land.

Kolkata Metropolitan Development Authority (KMDA) invited (March 2005)
expression of interest for development of Kolkata Logistic Hub at Kona, Howrah
under Public Private Partnership (PPP). With a view to relocate the wholesale
trading from the heart of the city the project envisaged construction of logistic
services such as truck parking, wholesale trading, warehousing and residential
accommodations for the site and ancillary staff. After evaluating the bids a
consortium’! was selected (October 2005) for implementation of the project on
106.35 acres of land (approximately) on 99 years’ lease in consideration of
% 103 crore. Audit scrutiny revealed irregularities in the transaction as well as
deviations in project objectives as detailed below:

> Development agreement (November 2005) executed between the two
parties inter alia stipulated that the parties would undertake a joint survey
immediately after signing the agreement to assess the quantum of land
actually handed over. In case the total area of the land was found to be
more or less than 106.35 acres, the consideration amount would be
adjusted accordingly. Audit scrutiny revealed that 108.43 acres of land
was found during the joint measurement and the same was handed over
(November 2006) to the consortium in consideration of ¥ 103.41 crore.
KMDA charged only 0.41 crore for additional 2.08 acres (108.43 acre
minus 106.35 acre) of land handed over to the agency against
₹ 2.01 crore” calculated at the agreed rate. Thus, undervaluation of land
rate in contravention of the agreement rendered in an undue benefit of
1.6 crore to the consortium.

> KMDA had no policy on transfer of land to the allottee where
constructional activities were prohibited. However, since 1999, KMDA
was charging 50 per cent of the existing land premium in respect of any
land falling under high tension zone where no construction was permitted.
Scrutiny revealed that an additional land of 2.0173 acres surrounded by
the allotted land (108.43 acres) was also handed over (November 2006) to
the agency free of cost as there was an underground IOC pipeline where
no construction activities were allowed. Subsequently, the Pricing
Committee of KMDA resolved (June 2008) that the principle of pricing
pattern for the land falling under high tension zone would be adopted in

> Contract agreement of the PPP envisaged construction of temporary

AGRICULTURE (MARKETING) DEPARTMENT

3.4.10 Avoidable loss under support price scheme

ज््य्यय््य्य्य्य्ययय्ज in the Government order coupled with delay of the Department in
communicating its decision to BENFED regarding disposal of potato led to
avoidable loss of = 5.59 crore in five districts under potato support price
scheme 2008.

To protect the interests of the farmers in the State as a result of large production
of potato in the season of 2008, the Agriculture Department (Marketing Branch)
decided, through a Government order of March 2008, to operationalise a ‘support
price scheme’ for immediate redressal of the anticipated distress sale of potatoes.
Under this scheme the Department sanctioned and released & 30 crore (€ 20 crore
in March 2008 and & 10 crore in April 2008) in favour of the West Bengal State
Co-operative Marketing Federation Limited (BEN FED)! as interest free
working capital loan for procurement of potato from common farmers at the rate
of % 250 per quintal. No condition for repayment of this loan by BENFED was,
however, laid down in the sanction orders. The orders operationalising the scheme
were silent about modalities of procurement of potato and their disposal, neither
was it clear whether further Government order was necessary to allow BENFED
to dispose off the potatoes.

State level apex body of the Primary Agriculture Marketing Societies

113


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

Accordingly, BENFED procured (April 2008) 8.28 lakh quintals of potato from
farmers of ten districts!! through primary level Co-operative Societies and stored
the procured potato in cold storages at an expenditure of € 25.03 crore.

BENFED, after three months from introduction of the scheme, moved only in
June 2008 the Agriculture Marketing Department for issue of instructions
regarding disposal of potato. The same was pursued by another communication in
August 2008. The Agriculture Department, after 49 days from the date of first
communication from BENFED, clarified (August 2008) that BENFED had to take
its commercial decision to dispose of the potato based on market price. BENFED
disposed (between August 2008 and January 2009) off the potato through sale of
bonds'™ at ₹ 9.64 crore (average rate हैं 117.27 per quintal) thereby incurring a
loss of € 15.39 crore. BENFED submitted (March 2009) an utilisation certificate
and requested Government for remission of loan liabilities to the extent of
%15.32 crore’? and deposited (August 2009) % 14.61 crore in Government
account. The decision of the Government regarding remission of हैं 15.32 crore
was awaited (October 2011).

Scrutiny (April-May 2011) of the records of five'™ district offices of BENFED
(out of ten) and the Directorate of Agricultural Marketing revealed that 7.74 lakh
quintals of potato procured in these districts under the scheme was disposed of
between August 2008 and January 2009 at prices ranging between & 30! and
₹ 160' per quintal generating a sale value of हैं 9.06 crore. Scrutiny further
revealed that in June 2008, when BENFED moved the Government for instruction
for disposal, the average monthly bond price in five districts was in the range of
% 165.88 and % 202.50 per quintal. Had the Department clarified its stand in the
original GO to state that BENFED had to take commercial decision in disposing
off the potato, BENFED would have sold the potatoes on time which would have
led to generation of sale proceeds of % 14.65 crore (based on the average bond
price of June 2008) and thereby minimising the loss by % 5.59 crore
(Appendix 3.8).

Agricultural Marketing Directorate stated (July 2011) that as no_ specific
guidelines were formulated by the Department for procurement operation,
BENFED had prepared their own guidelines to implement the scheme. The
Department also endorsed the reply, which indicates laxity of the Department in
determining policy and modalities of such price support scheme for ensuring
proper co-ordination among the Departments and various agencies.

10 Bankura, Birbhum, Bardhaman, Cooch Behar , Dakshin Dinajpur, Hooghly, Howrah, Jalpaiguri,

Paschim Medinipur and Murshidabad.

potato. The owner of the potato may sell the bond to others. Anyone who purchases the bond will have the

Thus, absence of modalities for support price scheme and lacunae in the
Government order coupled with delay of the Department in communicating its
decision to BENFED led to avoidable loss of हैं 5.59 crore (approximate) in five
districts under potato support price scheme 2008.

BENFED while reiterating the absence of clear departmental directives on this
issue, intimated (June 2011) that it was not feasible to find buyers of cold storage
receipts in June as potatoes stored in cold storage are released in late July/August.
The same is not acceptable as test check showed that cold storages in Hooghly
started releasing potato in the first week of May 2008. Further, it was evident
from the records of Agriculture Marketing Officers of the districts that buyers of
bonds were available even in June 2008.

It was further argued by BENFED that given the regulatory framework and social
accountability of its functioning, BENFED could not practice speculation for
harnessing higher sale price of potato. While appreciating the reply, the point still
remains that after safeguarding the potato-growers against distress sale of their
produce, efforts should have been made to protect the interest of Government by
minimizing the quantum of loss involved in the process. Moreover, the
department had acknowledged this fact since BENFED had been advised
belatedly to take action for disposal of potato, in keeping with commercial
interests.

Chapter 4: Chief Controlling Officer based Audit

DEPARTMENT

4 Functioning of Water Resources Investigation & Development
Department

Executive Summary

Government of West Bengal is responsible for planning, conservation,
development, utilisation and management of water resources in sustainable
manner for both the surface and groundwater in the Minor Irrigation (MI)
sector. The Department plays a pivotal role in ensuring assured irrigation for
agriculture by bringing more and more cultivable land under assured irrigation
with minimum time and investment. The Chief Controlling Officer based audit
of WRIDD was conducted to assess the performance of the Department during
the period 2006-11. Audit observed some areas of concern relating to
institutional, compliance and programme implementation, which affected the
performance of the Department and call for Government attention.

¢ With a view to reduce excessive stress on ground water the 11" Five
Year Plan envisaged special emphasis on utilisation of surface water
for creation of MI potential. The Department, however, continued to
depend more on ground water for creation of Irrigation Potential (IP)
in disregard to the plan proposal.

e Vacancies in key posts and inadequate infrastructure of the State Water
Investigation Directorate (SWID) adversely affected implementation of
West Bengal Ground Water Resources Act, 2005. The laboratories
were not properly equipped to analyse the chemical parameters
effectively due to non-availability of modern instruments,
non-functional equipments and irregular supply of chemicals/testing
kits. None of the laboratories was equipped for testing of heavy metals,
inorganic and organic pesticides, virological and bacteriological
analysis of water samples.

e Internal control mechanism of the Department was found deficient.
There was no departmental manual for guidance of the officers and
staff in carrying out their duties and for exercising internal control over
the activities of the Department.

e Water rates fixed were not in commensurate with the operation and
maintenance cost. Further, water rate of % 9.50 crore remained
unrealised.

e During 2006-11, 11990 MI schemes having command area 1.08 lakh
ha handed over to unregistered beneficiary committees. Despite huge
investment in these schemes the Department did not put an effective
mechanism in place for maintenance and monitoring of the schemes to
ensure optimum utilisation of created potential.

117


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

e Deficiencies in procurement, storage and accounting of materials were
noticed. Material was procured in excess of requirement which resulted
in piling of surplus materials for years.

¢ Utilisation of irrigation potential of government operated MI schemes
ranged between 29 per cent and 37 per cent during the period 2006-10
due to defunct schemes, reduction of designed command areas by
habitation, insufficient maintenance and irregular power supply. The
Department did not take effective step to revitalise the defunct
schemes.

e Lack of seriousness in implementation of the Act was noticed as
permits were not obtained from SWID prior to installation of 3705
groundwater based MI schemes by Water Resources Development
Directorate (WRDD). Workshops on mass awareness were conducted
only in eight districts during the first five years of implementation of
the Act.

4.1 Introduction

Agriculture is the primary occupation in West Bengal which contributes about
20 per cent of the Net State Domestic Product. About 70 per cent of the
population are dependent on agriculture for their livelihood. Minor Irrigation
(MI) has played a pivotal role in boosting agricultural production in the State
by bringing more and more land under assured irrigation round the year. MI
schemes!, which require less investment and low maintenance cost are more
acceptable to the farmers. The ultimate gross potential of MI schemes in the
State has been assessed at 44.34 lakh hectares (ha) against 26.83 lakh ha
through Major and Medium Irrigation (MMI) Schemes’. Total Irrigation
Potential (IP) created? upto 2008-09 was 38.99 lakh ha (72 per cent) under MI
schemes against 15.76 lakh ha (28 per cent) under MMI project.

The MI Schemes from both the surface water and groundwater are executed
and operated by the Water Resources Investigation and Development
Department (WRIDD). The Department is responsible for planning,
conservation, development, utilisation and management of water resources in
sustainable manner in the MI sector. The Government introduced ‘West
Bengal Ground Water Resources Management, Control and Regulation
(WBGWR) Act, 2005’ for conservation and management of groundwater,
which is also implemented by the Department.

4.2 Organisational structure

The Department is headed by a Secretary who is the Chief Controlling Officer
(CCO). There are two Directorates viz Water Resources Development
Directorate (WRDD) and State Water Investigation Directorate (SWID), four

ह Irrigation schemes having command area up to 2000 ha
? Irrigation schemes having command area more than 2000 ha

As per Economic Review 2009-10, published by Bureau of Applied Economics & Statistics, Development &
Planning Department, Government of West Bengal.

118


Chapter 4-Chief Controlling Officer based audit

Command Area Development Authorities’ (CADA) and two undertakings
namely West Bengal State Minor Irrigation Corporation Limited (WBSMICL)
and West Bengal Agro-Industries Corporation Limited (WBAICL) under this
Department (Appendix 4.1).

The function of WRDD is mainly to develop existing potential of water
resources available in the State for irrigation through construction and
maintenance of MI schemes? utilising groundwater by Agri-Irrigation (AI)
divisions as well as surface water by Agri-Mechanical (AM) divisions. This
Directorate also takes up various surface water diversion schemes, digging of
water harvesting tanks, drip, sprinkler and hydram schemes.

The SWID carries out investigation and assessment of surface water and
groundwater resources in the State. The WBGWR Act, 2005 and the Rules
made thereunder are implemented through SWID. It also provides technical
assistance in respect of feasibility of MI structures including augmentation of
water resources through various conservative/ artificial recharge measures.

The Command Area Development & Water Management (CADWM)
Programme was started from 1974-75 in Kangsabati, Mayurakshi and
Damodar Valley Projects and from 1999-2000 in Teesta Irrigation Project.
The primary objective of CADWM Programme was to bridge the gap between
the irrigation potential created through the canal network of the major
irrigation schemes and the area actually irrigated to make the best use of the
available land and water resources to increase agricultural production.

बज. Audit objectives
The audit was conducted to examine the overall functioning of the Department
and to assess whether

> the planning process and the manpower management was effective;
infrastructure and monitoring mechanism was adequate;

> the financial rules, orders and the procedures in budget preparation
were complied with;

> the operation & maintenance of the created schemes and material and
store management were efficient;

Vv

the service delivery in terms of execution and utilisation of MI
schemes and implementation of the WBGWR Act, 2005 were
effective.

4.4 Audit criteria

The audit findings were benchmarked against the following criteria:
> National Water Policy 2002

* Teesta CADA, Damodar Valley CADA, Kangsabati CADA and Mayurakshi CADA

° Heavy Duly Tube Wells (HDTW), Medium Duty Tube Wells (MDTW), Low Duty Tube Wells (DTW)
and Shallow Tube Wells (STW) in the ground water sector and Major RLI, Midi RLI, Mini RLI, Check
Dams ete in the surface water sector

119


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

> West Bengal Ground Water Resources (Management, Control and
Regulation) Act, 2005 and the Rules made thereunder

> Command Area Development and Water Management Programme
Guidelines

> Accelerated Irrigation Benefits Programme (AIBP) Guidelines, Rural
Infrastructure Development Fund (RIDF) Guidelines

> Instructions issued by the Government of West Bengal (GoWB) and

4.6 Institutional arrangements

With the objective of augmenting the existing irrigation potential as well as to
create additional potential and to investigate and assess water resources, a well
defined institutional arrangement of the Department is required. Efficient
planning based on reliable and current database, proper allocation of
budgetary and human resources, effective internal controls and monitoring etc.
ensure efficient functioning of the Department. Audit scrutiny revealed some
deficiencies in the institutional arrangements as discussed in the succeeding
paragraphs.

° Bankura, Bardhaman, Dakshin Dinajpur, East Medinipur, Jalpaiguri, Kolkata and Nadia

Chapter 4-Chief Controlling Officer based audit

4.6.1 Planning

For effective implementation of the MI schemes and the WBGWR Act, 2005
the Department was required to prepare a Five year plan, which was to be
staggered into Annual Action Plans (AAPs), based on current data base of
ground water and surface water resources, achievement of creation and
operation of MI schemes already planned/taken up etc. The deficiencies
noticed in audit are discussed below:

4.6.1.1 Fourth Minor Irrigation Census Report

Rationalisation of MI statistics was launched in 1986-87 by Government of
India to build a comprehensive and reliable database of number of MI
structures created and potential utilised through these schemes. Accordingly,
an all India census of MI structures was to be conducted on quinquennial
basis.

Audit observed that the 3™ MI Census with the reference year 2000-01 was
published in December 2003. The 47" MI census (2006-07) taken up in July
2007 was scheduled to be published by June 2010. However, a draft State
level report has been prepared only in October 2011. Thus the plans (2006-11)
prepared by the Department was based on the database of 3" MI Census.

The Director, SWID stated (July 2011) that the field work was delayed due to
shortage of manpower. Validation work suffered due to discrepancy of data
and rechecking of data upto village level which took much time.

4.6.1.2 District profile and estimation of dynamic ground water resources

As per section 9 of the Act, SWID was to prepare district wise profile of
ground water resources and district plan for conservation, maintenance and
utilisation of ground water for every five year period. However, scrutiny
revealed that district profiles and district plans were not prepared for any of
the districts in the State as of October 2011 even after six years of enactment
of the Act.

As per National Water Policy, 2002, the groundwater resources should be
re-assessed periodically. Estimation of dynamic groundwater resources in
West Bengal was made in March 2004 by SWID jointly with Central Ground
Water Board (CGWB). Out of total 341 blocks in the State, ground water
assessment was made in respect of 269 blocks excluding 72 saline infested
blocks (59 in the coastal area and 13 in northern hilly region). Fresh estimation
of dynamic ground water resources in the State was made in 2008-09. The
Report of the latest estimation was not finalised as of July 2011. Lack of
comprehensive and current data adversely affected the planning and execution
of the MI schemes. Further, Section 7 (3) of the Act provided issuance of
permits for sinking of new tube wells after scrutiny of the application forms in
terms of quantity and quality of ground water. Audit noticed that due to non
availability of latest assessment data, the SWID had to follow the
categorisation of block as per estimation of ground water resources done in
March 2004 while issuing permits for sinking of new tube wells. The
possibility of over exploitation of ground water in some areas can not be ruled
out and the whole exercise of issuing permits for sinking of new tube wells
based on assessment of ground water availability was rendered infructuous.

4.6.1.3 Deficiencies in planning

The Five year plan (2007-12) and the Annual plans for the years 2006-07 to
2010-11 were prepared by the Department based on data of 3“ MI census. The
plans prepared by the Department were based on top down approach as
necessary inputs were not obtained from the field offices. Besides, the targets
were fixed in the Five Year Plans and Annual plans on lump sum basis
without any details and proper analysis of the previous years’
achievement/shortfall. The Department, in reply, stated (November 2011) that
the Annual plans were prepared after considering achievements in the previous
year. The reply was, however, not tenable as the targets were fixed by the
Department unrealistically during 2006-11 despite huge shortfalls in the
previous year (as discussed in para 4.8.1).

As per assessment of dynamic ground water resources conducted in March
2004, 38 blocks out of 341 blocks in the State were categorised as ‘Critical’
and ‘Semi-critical’ because of significant decline of water level. With a view
to reducing excessive exploitation on ground water, 117 Five Year Plan
(2007-12) envisaged special emphasis on utilisation of surface water through
implementation of River Lift Irrigation (RLI) schemes, construction of water
harvesting tanks, surface flow MI schemes and some new schemes such as
drip, sprinkler and hydram.

Chapter 4-Chief Controlling Officer based audit

4.6.1.4 Lack of preliminary survey and investigation in selection of schemes

Irrigation schemes were required to be taken up after carrying out detailed
survey of availability and requirement of water considering hydrological,
environmental and ecological aspects of the schemes. As per guidelines for
issue of water availability certificate for RLI schemes, SWID was required to
consider data of three years to assess the sustainability of the water sources.

The proposals of the new schemes are investigated by the departmental
engineers for preparation of the techno feasibility reports after obtaining
clearance of availability of required water from SWID. The list of feasible
schemes is submitted to District Site Selection Committee (DSSC) for
approval. Approved schemes are taken up by the Department for execution
under fund from Rural Infrastructure Development Fund (RIDF), Accelerated
Irrigation Benefit Programme (AIBP) and State Plan.

The Department took up 14,046 schemes under RIDF-VIII to RIDF-XV and
AIBP-Phase-I during 2002-10 of which 131 schemes having an estimated cost
of & 22.31 crore were dropped after approval (Appendix 4.3). Further, sites of
102 MI schemes under RIDF projects’ were subsequently changed which
resulted in delay in completion.

The Department attributed (November 2011) non-availability of suitable
aquifer, change of river courses and dried up water sources as reasons for
dropping of the schemes after approval. Scrutiny, however, revealed that
SWID considered the water availability data of a particular date against the
prevailing norms of three consecutive years before issuing water availability
certificate towards installation of RLI schemes. Thus, adequate survey and
investigations were not conducted before submission of proposals for
construction of the MI schemes.

.6.2 Manpower Management

The performance of any organisation and efficient implementation of
programmes depends on availability of qualified and trained manpower. Audit
scrutiny revealed that the Department did not carry out assessment of its
manpower requirement, taking into account the present and future needs to
fulfil its objectives. There were vacancies in key posts including 93 Assistant
Engineers, 230 Sub-Assistant Engineers, 329 Mechanic/Draftsman/Surveyor
and 3326 operators (Appendix 4.4) which affected the performance and
service delivery, maintenance of important records, monitoring of handed over
schemes, reconciliation of electric bills etc. The Department replied
(November 2011) that assessment of man-power requirement was underway,
taking into account of the future needs and exact competencies.

Further, as per departmental guidelines, transfer of an officer or staff shall be
on expiry of five years of service at a particular place. In 11 test checked
divisions, it was noticed that 50 employees out of 160 employees remained
posted in the same division for a period in excess of five years in violation of

” RIDF-X, XI, XI and XH

departmental guidelines (Appendix 4.5). Periodical change of posting of

The Assistant Engineers posted in sub-divisions under WRDD have to
perform the functions of Drawing and Disbursing Officers (DDOs). Therefore,
induction training to newly recruited Assistant Engineers is essential for
discharging the duties of DDOs and also for day-to-day technical work.
Scrutiny of records of the WRDD, however, revealed that 71 Assistant
Engineers were recruited during 2006-11 through West Bengal Public Service
Commission. However, no training was imparted to make them aware of their
duties and responsibilities before posting to different field units just after
recruitment. In the exit conference the Secretary, WRIDD admitted
(October 2011) the audit observation.

4.6.2.1 Idling of drilling units in SWID

Four Drilling divisions® along with 15 sub-divisions under the Drilling circle
of SWID were created (1975) with the objective to investigate and explore the
ground water resources through drilling of tube-wells. However, no
exploratory drilling work was conducted by the divisions after June 2000. As a
result, man and machineries engaged with the drilling work remained idle
since then. Not only work of drilling exploratory tube-wells has been
suspended but also installations of MI schemes were hampered due to
non-availability of rig machines. Conditions of the existing rig machines and
other machineries under those divisions had deteriorated to a great extent
requiring thorough overhauling, major repairing and replacement of major
spare parts. A proposal for revival of the Drilling Units at an estimated cost of
% 63.70 lakh was submitted (December 2009) by the Director, SWID. The
Department neither took any action to repair the drilling machineries nor
deployed 25 idle staff including 11 technical staff engaged in those divisions
elsewhere for their gainful utilisation. Consequently, expenditure of
= 7.53 crore incurred (during 2006-11) on pay and allowances’ of the idle staff
was unfruitful. The Department stated (November 2011) that the exploration
works of drilling was being outsourced presently and the Drilling circle would
be wound up gradually. The idle staff were being transferred to the other
divisions in a phased manner.

4.6.3 Inadequate infrastructure of SWID

WBGWR Act, 2005 provides for creation of three tiers of authorities i.e. State
Level Ground Water Resources Development Authority (SLA) as the Apex
body, Corporation Level Ground Water Resources Development Authority
(CLA) for the Kolkata Municipal Area and District Level Ground Water
Resources Development Authorities (DLA) for each of the 18 districts for
implementation of the WBGWR Act, 2005. Accordingly, SWID has been
declared as the functional organ of the SLA. The Director, SWID has been
made (September 2005) the ex-officio Chairman of the SLA and the
Superintending Geologist of the Geological Circle of SWID is the

4.6.3.1 Inadequate infrastructure of laboratories

Qualitative assessment of ground water resources of the State through
periodical analysis, study and review of the physical, chemical, bacteriological
and viorological qualities was to be conducted by the laboratories under
SWID. Audit scrutiny revealed that prior to enactment of the WBGWR Act,
2005, there were five'! Chemical and Hydrological laboratories under SWID
for monitoring the quality of ground water. To strengthen the Chemical Wing
of SWID eight'* new laboratories were sanctioned in September 2006 out of
which three’? laboratories could not be made functional (June 2011) due to
non-deployment of manpower. There was also shortage of manpower'* both
skilled and un-skilled in the existing laboratories. The laboratories were not in
a position to analyse chemical parameters effectively due to non-availability of
modern instruments'°, non-functional _ instruments (Appendix 4.6) and
irregular supply of chemicals/testing kits. Records of Central Laboratory,
SWID revealed that in absence of modern instruments, impurities like arsenic
and iron were tested by test kits which did not produce accurate results.
Further, none of the laboratories in the State was equipped for testing of heavy
metals, inorganic and organic pesticides, virological and bacteriological
analysis of water samples as envisaged in the Act. The Department stated
(November 2011) that upgrading the testing capabilities of the laboratories

4.6.4 Internal Controls and Monitoring

Monitoring at various levels of governance provides assurance of the
reliability of reporting in the Department. Besides, these controls enable the
Department to identify the key problem areas, constraints and managerial
needs for the improvement in policy formulation, allocation of resources and
setting of performance standards. Audit observed the following:

4.6.4.1 Departmental Manual not prepared

Departmental Manual infer alia stipulates rules and regulations, procedures,
instructions, responsibility structures, control mechanisms and _ prescribed
formats and periodicity of report returns to be submitted to the appropriate
authorities and also provide guidelines for execution of schemes/projects. This
is essential for guidance of the officers and staff in carrying out their duties
and also for exercising proper internal control over activities of the
Department.

Scrutiny revealed that the Department issued administrative orders and
guidelines on its various functions. However, the Department had not prepared
any Departmental Manual till date (November 2011). Audit observed that
there was no clear cut demarcation of functional responsibility at various
levels, laid down procedures of monitoring of groundwater level, collection
and preservation of water samples, procedures of testing of different
parameters of ground and surface water for irrigation purposes.

The Department stated (November 2011) that the orders and guidelines were
issued time to time to bring uniformity in practices and procedures. In the exit
conference the Secretary, WRIDD stated that the Financial and Technical
Manuals prepared under WBADMIP would be followed in the Department.
The Department also stated that a manual for preparation of Scheme
Development and Management Plan was under preparation.

4.6.4.2 Annual inspection of field units

Periodical inspection by the departmental officers is an important and effective
tools to ensure proper functioning of the ficld units. Rules 161 and 162 of
WBER provides that Superintending Engineers (SEs) and Executive Engineers
(EEs) were required to conduct annual inspection of divisions and
sub-divisions under their respective control.

Audit scrutiny, however, revealed that 21 out of 24 test checked divisions'®
were not inspected by the concerned SEs and 66 sub-divisions out of 81
(under the 24 test checked divisions) were not inspected by the concerned EEs
during 2006-11. Thus, the compliance by the divisional and sub divisional
officers with the prescribed rules, procedures and instructions and also
maintenance of important records and registers were not ensured by the SEs
and EEs.

'6 20Divisions of WRDD and 4 Divisions of SWID

Chapter 4-Chief Controlling Officer based audit

Important records
like Instruction
Book, Assets
