Report Register not
maintained

The Department stated (November 2011) that instructions have been issued to
ensure regular inspection of the field units and submission of reports to the
Department for necessary evaluation.

4.6.4.3 Non-maintenance of important registers/records

Para 26.2 of Central Public Works Departmental Manual provides for
maintenance of site instruction book by the contractor to note down
instructions issued from time to time by the departmental authority during site
visits. The book should be submitted to the division along with the final bill to
monitor whether the instructions issued were followed by the contractors.
Scrutiny of records, however, revealed that site instruction books were not
issued to the contractors in 19 test checked divisions (Appendix 4.7).

As per Government order (June 2006) all administrative departments
including their field offices are required to maintain Assets Register with
immediate effect. WBFR also stipulates that each divisional officer will keep a
register of all immovable Government property including land and buildings
within his division. Scrutiny revealed that 15 test checked divisions
(Appendix 4.7) did not maintain Assets Registers. As a result, the management
was unaware of the actual status of the assets at their disposal, encroachment,
if any, and their safe custody. Further, Survey Report Register and Theft case
file were also not maintained by 13 and 12 test checked divisions respectively.
The Department stated (November 2011) that records would be maintained as
per Public Works Department Rules.

4.6.4.4 Absence of Internal Audit

Internal audit is an important tool for the management to ascertain the
accuracy and effectiveness of the internal control mechanism. There was no
separate internal audit wing in the Department. Internal audit wing of the
Finance Department also did not conduct any audit in any of the test checked
divisions during 2006-11. In reply, the Department stated (November 2011)
that the proposal to create a separate internal audit wing was in active
consideration.

4.6.4.5 Response to Government audit

Periodical inspection of the field units of the Department is conducted by the
Audit team to test check the transactions and verify the maintenance of the
important accounts and records as prescribed in the rules and procedures. The
irregularities noticed in the inspections are followed up with the inspection
reports (IRs) incorporating irregularities detected during the inspection. IRs
are issued to the heads of offices inspected with copies to the next higher
authorities for taking prompt corrective action and report their compliance to
us.

It was, however, noticed that out of the IRs issued between April 2006 and
March 2011, 180 paragraphs relating to 57 IRs remained outstanding as of
March 2011 (Appendix 4.8) for want of comments of the Government,
non-regularisation of expenditure etc. Further, 90 paragraphs related to 46 IRs
issued between April 1984 and March 2006 remained outstanding.

Meetings of the Audit committee, comprising the Secretary of the Department

settlement of the outstanding paragraphs and IRs.

Lack of response to audit observations depicted lack of proper accountability
mechanism on the part of the Department. The Department stated
(November 2011) that a special drive to settle the outstanding paragraphs
would be undertaken.

4.7 Compliance Issues

4.7.1 Budget and financial reporting

Control over budget and expenditure is essential for optimal utilisation of
limited resources to achieve the objectives. The shortcomings noticed in
control over preparation of the budget and expenditure thereof are discussed in
the succeeding paragraphs:

4.7.1.1 Preparation of budget

One of the basic tenets of efficient financial management is preparation of
realistic budget. West Bengal Budget Manual stipulates that budget estimates
are to be prepared by the Chief Engineer (CE) and to be submitted by
1 November to the Secretary, WRIDD who will in turn submit the same to the
Finance Department by 1 December of each year. For this purpose, the EEs of
the working divisions are to submit their budget estimates to the SEs by
15 September each year and the SEs are to prepare circle-wise budget
estimates and submit the same to the CE by 1 October each year.

Contrary to the provision, budget estimates were prepared by the CE-I of
WRDD in consultation with the other CEs of WRDD, Director of SWID and
Administrator of Teesta CADA'* and Managing Director of WBSMICL and
submitted the same to the Secretary, WRIDD after a delay of 90 to 120 days
during 2006-11.

Scrutiny revealed that the circles and divisions of WRDD did not prepare any
budget estimate during 2006-11 for submission to the higher authorities.
Similarly, the circles of SWID prepared annual budget estimates during
2006-10 without any inputs from the divisions and submitted the same after a
delay of 3 to 86 days, as detailed in (Appendix 4.9). Three circles’? did not
furnish the budget estimates for 2010-11 at all while the Geological circle
submitted the budget estimates after a delay of 190 days for the year.

Thus, the budget was prepared without of inputs from the lower formations as
required under the Budget Manual. This was indicative of laxity in budgetary
control by the Department. The Department stated (November 2011) that the

procedure for preparation of the budget would be streamlined from 12" Five

4.7.2__ Compliance with financial laws, regulations and rules

For sound financial administration and financial control, it is essential that
expenditure conforms to the financial rules, regulations and orders issued by
the competent authority. This not only prevents _ irregularities,
misappropriation and frauds, but also helps in maintaining good financial
discipline. Instances of non-compliance with rules and regulations noticed in
audit are discussed as under:

4.

As per Rule 389 A of WBFR, rush of expenditure particularly in the closing
month of the financial year is regarded as a breach of financial regularity.
Scrutiny of records of 23 divisions 2 out of 30 selected divisions revealed that
31 to 50 per cent of the expenditure was incurred by the divisions in March
during 2006-2011 as evident from table below:

1 Rush of expenditure

Table 4.2 Expenditure in March during 2006-2011 (Rupees in crore)
= ———— Total expenditure | Expenditure in March | Percentage of expenditure in March

2006-2007 57.26 28.85 50

2007-2008 79.49 28.81 36

2008-2009 84.45 39.65 47

2009-2010 86.55 34.87 40

2010-2011 65.75 20.23 31

(Source: compiled from records of test checked divisions)

Further, scrutiny of records revealed that the funds released by the
Directorates through Letter of Credit (LOC) to these divisions during the last
month of the financial year ranged between 18 to 45 per cent of the total fund
released during the respective financial year. Fifty one per cent to 98 per cent
LOC released in March was issued after 15" March every year. Release of
funds by the Directorates at the fag end of the year was the main reason for
rush of expenditure. Thus uniform flow of expenditure during the year, a
primary requirement of budgetary control, was not followed indicating
deficient financial management. The Department stated (November 2011) that
maximum fund was utilised at the end of the financial year as works were
usually executed during November to March of the year as per availability of
sites.

Chapter 4-Chief Controlling Officer based audit

4.7.2.2. Non-reconciliation of issue of cheques and remittances

In terms of Para 22.3.1 of Central Public Works Accounts Code, public works
divisions are required to furnish to the Accounts Office details of cheques
drawn, cheques encashed and balance of cheques remaining uncashed in
Form-51 “schedule of settlement with treasury”-Part-II, duly supported with
“Certificate of Treasury Issues” (CTIs) provided by the Treasury Officer.
Similarly, the divisions are also to furnish “schedule of settlement with
treasuries” in Form-51-Part-I, duly supported with Form-50 “Consolidated
Treasury Receipts” (CTRs) showing therein the amount remitted to the
treasuries, amount acknowledged by the treasuries and balance remaining
unacknowledged at the end of each month. It was, however, noticed that out of
24 test-checked divisions having cheque drawing power, in 14 divisions
CTIs and CTRs were in arrear for the period prior to 2006-11. As regular
reconciliation gives the opportunity of timely detection of irregularities like
missing credit, bounced cheques, misappropriation of departmental receipts,
fraudulent drawal of funds etc., absence of reconciliation leaves the scope of
such irregularities.

As per provisions of the WBGWR Rules 2006, any user desiring to sink a
tubewell is required to deposit the application fee in the treasury and submit a
copy of the treasury challan to the DLA/CLA in support of payment of
application fee. Scrutiny of records of three Geological divisions’ under
SWID revealed that there was no system of cross verification of the copy of
the treasury challan with the records of the treasury, leaving the scope of
submission of fake challans. The Secretary, WRDD in the exit conference
(November 2011) accepted the audit contention of non-reconciliation of
amount remitted to Treasury/Bank and assured to take necessary steps in this
regard.

4.7.2.3 Lapsed deposits not credited to the government account

West Bengal Treasury Rules, 2005, provides that all deposits or balances in
excess of % 25 unclaimed for more than three complete accounting years after
the year of deposit should be credited to the Government account as ‘lapsed
deposit’. In 15 test checked divisions”> of WRDD, security deposits amounting
to हैं 28.11 lakh deducted from the agencies up to March 2008 and remained
unclaimed for more than three years had not been credited to Government
account as ‘lapsed deposit’ as of March 2011, in violation of the above
provision. The Department accepted (November 2011) that the unclaimed
amount is to be deposited in the Government account as a lapsed deposit.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

4.7.3__Lack of control over collection of water rates

As per National Water Policy (2002), water charges should be fixed in such a
way so that the operation and maintenance charges are covered for
sustainability of the created assets. Scrutiny, however, revealed that in 15 test
checked divisions of WRDD which operated and maintained MI schemes,
water rates actually realised was 31 per cent to 43 per cent of the operating
cost of the schemes during the period 2006-10. Water rates for supply of
irrigation water through MI schemes under the State Government were last
revised in July 2003. Thus, the water rate fixed by the Department did not
commensurate with the actual operation and maintenance cost of the MI
schemes.

Further, realisation of water rates against supply of irrigation water from the
MI installations of WRDD are being done by the different Block Development
Officers (BDOs) in the State. The beneficiaries were to deposit water rates in
advance to the BDOs for availing irrigation facilities. On production of the
prepaid receipt issued by the BDOs, operators of the schemes record the same
in their log book and release water accordingly. Scrutiny, however, revealed
that water rates amounting to % 9.50 crore remained unrealised as of
March 2011 (Appendix 4.10). In reply, the EEs stated (March to June 2011)
that water rates were collected by the concerned BDOs and they had no
control over collection of water rates. However, the fact that the BDO
functioned as a mere collector and imposition of water rates and release of
water for irrigation after verifying paid challans were the responsibilities of
the concerned divisions of the WRDD. There was no coordination between the
divisions and BDOs in this regard. The poor collection, in comparison to the
amount due, was indicative of inadequate control and monitoring by the
WRDD.

The Department stated (November 2011) that necessary steps would be taken
to augment collection of revenue.

4.7.4.2 Handed over schemes

As per RIDF guidelines and departmental notifications, MI schemes on
completion are to be handed over to the concerned Gram Panchayat (GP)/
Panchayat Samity (PS) for subsequent handing over to the Beneficiary
Committees (BCs) for operation and maintenance. Cost of operation and
maintenance including payment of electricity bills is to be borne by the BCs
concerned. However, all the assets of “handed over schemes” would continue
to be owned by the Department. The concerned divisional engineers are
responsible for looking after the assets without infringing upon the
management initiative of the BCs and also for imparting technical know-how
including training to the beneficiaries on how the installations to be handled
and maintained properly so as to yield optimal benefit to all the concerned.
Further, the divisional engineers are required to obtain necessary feedback on
performance of such handed over schemes and prepare a comprehensive report
for submission to the CE, WRDD.

Upto 2010-11, WRDD handed over 38599 MI schemes having command area
11990 Minor of 2.86 lakh ha to the BCs through the respective GPs for operation and
Irrigation Schemes maintenance. Out of this, 11990 schemes having command area of 1.08 lakh
having command _ ha were handed over to the unregistered BCs during 2006-11. The Department
area of 1.08 lakh ha have no data regarding number of registered BCs. Raising of electricity bill in

4.7.5 Material and store management

A sound system of stores management involves planning of purchase
requirements, efficient and economic procurement, control over issue, timely
and proper accounting and safe physical custody of stores. Audit noticed the
following deficiencies in inventory management as discussed below:

4.7.5.1 Deficiency in accounting of stores

A large part of the estimated cost of MI Schemes is the material cost. Audit
scrutiny of 15 test checked divisions” of WRDD revealed that no Suspense Head
for ‘Stock’ was operated and expenditure incurred on procurement of material
was booked directly to the respective scheme head or maintenance head. As per
CPWA Code, material-at-site accounts for every individual work was required to
be maintained when expenditure was directly booked against the final head of
accounts. Scrutiny revealed that none of the test checked divisions maintained
material-at-site accounts. As a result, value of excess materials of a particular
scheme remained unutilised in the store and the booked expenditure of the work
remained inflated. However, store ledgers were maintained in sub-divisional
stores for keeping account of the quantity of the materials. “Bin Cards” and
“Price Store Ledger” showing quantity and value of material were also not
maintained and as such value of material available with the Department could not
be ascertained in audit.

As per Rule 244 of WBFR, material which is no longer required in the work
should be disposed off by transfer or sale. However, it was seen in audit that
proper account of surplus/unserviceable/obsolete stores were not maintained in
any of the test checked divisions. In absence of such mechanism, total value of
such stores in the Department and their disposal could not be ascertained in
audit.

4.7.5.2 Excess procurement

Rule 101 of WBFR provides that procurement of stores should be made in the
most economical manner in accordance with the requirement. Accordingly,
requirement of material is to be assessed on the basis of the detailed project
reports prepared after proper survey and investigation of individual schemes.

Funds for the procurement of material were directly provided to the Resources
Divisions*', Material procured by these divisions was to be kept in their
sub-divisional stores and should be lifted by the working divisions from those
stores as per requirement. Audit scrutiny revealed that materials worth
% 6.63 crore procured for RIDF-X to XIII between December 2005 and
November 2008 were lying unutilised (January 2011) in the sub-divisional stores
of Kolkata (A-M) Resources Division, due to non-lifting of materials by the
working divisions. The materials were not lifted as the schemes were dropped
subsequently.

Resources Division)

Audit scrutiny further revealed that in seven divisions’? out of 15 test checked
working divisions of WRDD, surplus materials viz. submersible pumps,
UPVC™ pipes, ता pipes ete. worth हैं 2.41 crore procured for implementation
of the MI schemes in between March 2004 and November 2007 were lying
unutilised as of March 2011. The surplus materials were not transferred to
other divisions even after lapse of three to seven years. The possibility of these
stores becoming obsolete could not be ruled out. Audit scrutiny revealed that
materials for implementation of MI schemes were procured on the basis of
estimates of model schemes. This resulted in accumulation of surplus materials in
the stores of the working divisions.

The Department considering the cascading effect of such stockpiling and the
resultant wastage and loss of materials in store, issued (September 2011) an
order which inter alia stipulated a time frame to prepare an inventory of the
serviceable materials in the stock, issue on the basis of ‘First in First Out
method’, disposal of damaged stock as per Government guidelines. The said
order, thus, corroborates our audit contention that procurement of material was
not in accordance with requirement.

4.7.5.3 Physical verification of stores

As per Rule-137 of WBFR, divisional officers should conduct annual physical
verification of the material at least once a year by an independent officer.
Scrutiny, however, revealed that none of the 15 test checked divisions’ of
WRDD conducted annual physical verification of stores regularly. In nine test
checked divisions material worth Rs 55.93 lakh were stolen during 2006-11
from different godowns and schemes sites. Although FIRs were lodged with
the Police authorities, final investigation reports had not been received as of
March 2011. No action had been initiated by the Department as of March 2011
to investigate the cases and fix up the responsibilities. Thus, in absence of
regular physical verification of stores and incidence of theft of store materials
pilferage of more materials could not be ruled out. The Department stated
(November 2011) that necessary instructions in this matter had already been
issued. However, the reply was not tenable as the instructions issued
(September 2011) were for preparation of only an inventory of excess material
accumulated in the stores for disposal.

4.7.5.4 Absence of adequate infrastructure for storage of materials

As per Rule-109 of WBER, the divisional officer is responsible for proper
arrangements for the custody of stores and for their protection from
deterioration and fire.

Scrutiny, however, revealed that huge quantity of material viz UPVC pipes
with fittings, strainers, generators etc. was kept in open in stack yard/ office
compound for years together which resulted in rapid deterioration. Also,
chances of pilferage could not be ruled out. As per view of the Committee of
CEs of WRDD (2005) for procurement of pipes, UPVC pipes attain brittleness
in three to six months due to exposure of ultraviolet rays of sun. In spite of the
observance of the Committee, huge quantities of UPVC pipes alongwith
fittings were kept in open as is evident from the following picture:

Diesel Engines kept in open in the compound of PYC pipes and iron strainer in open compound of

In view of the above the Department needs to take necessary steps for

4.8.1.2 Use of ground water in the quality affected areas

Scientific studies revealed that lowering of water table have a direct
relationship with arsenic contamination in water and its adverse effect on
agricultural products. Out of 19 districts in the State, 12 are affected with
fluoride or arsenic. Scrutiny revealed that there were 19012 government
operated ground water based MI schemes in 124 blocks of 12 districts affected
with arsenic or fluoride. In three test checked arsenic or fluoride affected
districts*', 1044 MI structures were installed in the affected blocks of the
districts during the period 2006-11, leaving human beings and cattle exposed
to the risk of arsenic/fluoride contamination from consumption of agricultural
products in those affected areas. The Department stated (November 2011) that
there was no restriction in using arsenic/fluoride contaminated water in
agriculture, however, safe and deeper aquifer was being harnessed in the
arsenic affected blocks. The reply was, however, not tenable as SWID decided
(April 2009) not to install tube well in arsenic and fluoride affected areas in
case of West Bengal Accelerated Development Minor Irrigation Project.

4.8.1.3 Non-conversion of diesel operated schemes

Operation and maintenance (O&M) of electric operated schemes is more
economical, convenient and environment friendly than that of diesel operated
schemes. The O&M of diesel operated schemes on the other hand involved
lots of hazards like carriage and storage of highly inflammable High Speed
Diesel and lube-oil. The Budget proposal of WRIDD of 2009-10 is also
emphasised conversion to electrified schemes to cut down the escalating
operation cost. The CE, WRDD also opined (June 2008) that non-plan
expenditure towards procurement of diesel and lubricants exceeded the budget
allocation due to constant escalation of the price of diesel and lubricants.
Scrutiny, however, revealed that 1483 (44 percent) out of 3366

WBSEDCL in March 1999.

During 2006-11, the Department incurred an expenditure of = 3.09 crore
towards carriage, storage and security on procurement of HSD oil and
lubricants. Scrutiny further revealed that in six * out of seven test checked
Agri Mechanical Divisions, 2.26 lakh litre of HSD oil worth = 77.97 lakh was
diverted for running government vehicles and office generators
unauthorisedly.

4.8.2 Functioning of State Water Investigation Directorate

4.8.2.1 Implementation of WBGWR Act, 2005

SWID acts as the functional organ of the SLA for implementation of
WBGWR Act, 2005. The main functions of the SLA are as follows:

> To review quantitative and qualitative analysis of ground water,

Vv

To manage the ground water resources including issue of certificate for
ground water extraction,

> To initiate a policy to conserve the ground water resources by way of
recharging, replenishing, recycling etc. and
> To create mass awareness regarding conservation and management of

ground water.

A review of the functioning of the SWID, as enshrined under the Act, revealed
the following:

. Periodical quantitative and qualitative monitoring of Ground water

SWID monitors block-wise depth of ground water level and its fluctuation
throughout the State in four seasons“ of a year through a net work of 2082

22 Ralurghat (AM) Division-57, Kalyani (AM) Division-14 and Krishnagar (AM) Division-28
 Balurghat (AM), Bankura (AM), Bardhaman (AM), Jalpaiguri (AM), Krishnagar (AM) and Tamluk
(AM)

+ Pre monsoon (April ); Monsoon ( August ); Post monsoon (November) and Winter (January)

141


Reduce Level
Survey of PHSs
not completed in
four out of seven
selected districts

Mass awareness
workshops were
not conducted in
eight out of 18
districts during
the first five years
of implementation
of WBGWR Act,
2005

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

Permanent Hydrograph Stations (PHSs). The data so collected are processed
and fluctuation of water level is estimated. Scrutiny, however, revealed that
periodical monitoring of water level was not done regularly for all the PHSs in
the test checked districts except Jalpaiguri due to 72 PHSs remaining defunct,
inaccessible sites etc. as shown in Appendix 4.12. As a result, proper
monitoring of ground water level and its fluctuation could not be ensured.

Further, “Reduced Level Survey” for detection of elevation from the “Mean
Sea Level” for all PHSs is necessary for determining ground water flow
direction and for estimation of ground water resources. In absence of such
survey, contour maps of ground water table cannot be prepared. Scrutiny of
records revealed that “Reduced Level Survey” had not been completed in
Bankura, Bardhaman, Dakshin Dinajpur and East Medinipur out of seven
selected districts.

Block-wise water samples are also collected twice** a year from the existing
PHSs or from nearby wells and analysed at the laboratories of SWID. In
addition to routine monitoring of chemical quality of ground water arsenic and
fluoride contamination were also tested. Scrutiny of records of four test
checked laboratories“, however, revealed that none of these laboratories
conducted testing of samples as per the parameters envisaged in the WBGWR
Act, 2005 as discussed in paragraph 4.6.3.1. The Senior Chemist/Chemist of
these laboratories stated that qualitative analysis of the ground water could not
be done as per the WBGWR Act, 2005 due to irregular supply of chemicals/
testing kits, lack of modern instruments, non-functional instruments, shortage
of manpower.

. Mass Awareness Programme

As per the WBGWR Act, 2005 the SLA was required to create mass
awareness and encourage interaction between modern technologies and
age-old practices of ground water conservation and management by
harnessing traditional knowledge and dovetailing such traditional knowledge
with modern technologies. Audit scrutiny revealed that only eight mass
awareness workshops in eight’” district headquarters out of total 18 districts
had been conducted up to March 2011 to make people aware about the ground
water quality, quantity and necessity of its conservation. As a result, the
objective to sensitise the mass regarding the problems and conservation of
ground water was not fully achieved.

e Slow progress in respect of issuance of permits and Certificates of
Registration

The WBGWR Act, 2005 stipulates obtaining permit from SLA/DLA/CLA
against payment of prescribed fees for extraction of ground water by
mechanical means. The WBGWR Rules, 2006 also provides that all existing
MI installations constructed for extraction of ground water prior to the date of

non-awareness amongst the users.

The Secretary, WRIDD in the exit conference stated (November 2011) that
last date of extension up to July 2011 had been expired and Government Order
had been issued to do away with the process of registration. The Department,
however, failed to furnish the said order subsequently though called for.

Thus, the main objective of the WBGWR Act, 2005 to manage, control and
regulate indiscriminate extraction of ground water in the State was frustrated.

4.8.3 Activities of Command Area Development Authorities

In West Bengal, IP of four major river valley projects was created by the
Irrigation and Waterways Department but the CADWM programme in the
command area of these river valley projects was implemented by the
Department through four CADAs. Fifty per cent cost is borne by the GOI with
the condition that the beneficiary farmers should contribute 10 per cent of the
cost and the balance 40 per cent is to be borne by the State Government.

4.8.3.1 Shortfall in utilisation of created Irrigation Potential

The components of the CADWMA Programme are Survey, Planning, Design,
On-farm development (OFD) works including construction of field channels
and reclamation of water logged areas etc. CADAs, however, concentrated its
activities on construction of field channels to bridge the gap between the
created and utilised Culturable Command Area (CCA). Year-wise targets and

achievement in respect of coverage of CCA as informed by the CADAs, are

shown in Table below:

Table 4.8: Targets and achievement of CADAs during 2006-11

Year Target Achievement Shortfall Shortfall
(in Ha) (in Ha) (in Ha) percentage

2006-07 6600 3599.75 3000.25 45
2007-08 6600 2437.25 4162.75 63
2008-09 9930 6741.00 3189.00 32
2009-10 11600 8206.00 3394.00 29
2010-11 9200 2488.10 6711.90 73

(Source ; Information furnished by CADAS)

From the above table it was evident that there was shortfall in achievement
ranging between 29 per cent and 73 per cent during 2006-11.

Scrutiny of records of two test checked divisions’ of CADAs revealed that
out of 435 field channel schemes taken up during 2006-11, only 267 schemes
were completed, 131 schemes were ongoing and 37 schemes were dropped.

The concerned EEs stated (April and May 2011) that the schemes were
dropped mainly due to reluctance of agencies to continue the work,
non-approval of revised estimate and land problem. The progress of the works
was also affected due to intermittent fund flow.

Further, as per the CADWM Programme guidelines, properly planned, aligned
and designed field channels should be constructed from outlet in such a
manner that the entire command area of the outlet is connected by field
channels for efficient utilisation of irrigation water. Scrutiny of records of
Damodar Valley CAD division revealed that 53 schemes were declared
completed or abandoned midway during 2006-11 even though the executed

30 Kangsabati CAD Division, Bankura and Damodar Valley CAD Division, Bardhaman

could not

Chapter 4-Chief Controlling Officer based audit

lengths of lined (pucca) and unlined (kuchcha) field channel were 26 per cent
and 48 per cent less than the estimated lengths respectively. As a result,
1401 ha of command area against the target of 6489 ha could not be covered
by construction of field channels. The EE, Damodar Valley CAD Division
stated that non-achievement of targeted length was mainly due to land dispute
and poor infrastructure of Gram Panchayats (GPs) through which works were
executed. The reply is not acceptable as the problems should have been
foreseen before taking up the field channel schemes. Besides, despite large
investment shortfall in creation of field channels deprived the targeted
beneficiaries.

4.8.3.2 Participatory Irrigation Management

MoWR (Gol) circulated (1998) a model Act to be adopted by the State
Legislatures for enacting new Irrigation Acts/amending the existing Irrigation
Acts for facilitating “Participatory Irrigation Management (PIM)”. It was
necessary for active involvement of users for equitable distribution of
irrigation water, maintenance of distribution network, payment of water
charges and checking of unauthorised irrigation. However, no such Act was
enacted in West Bengal as of November 2011. As per the guidelines of
CADWM, formation of Water Users Associations (WUAs) was mandatory for
each scheme involving the beneficiaries. The WUAs were to be registered for
monitoring of distribution of water in the field channels and maintenance of
the schemes handed over to them. As per information furnished by CADAs,
only 293 WUAs were registered out of 547 formed as of March 2010, as
shown in the table below:

Table 4.9: CADA-wise Water User’s Association formed and registered

SL | Name of Number of Water Users’ Association (as of 31.03.2010)
iim || 825 | iret eserrrtie || meariontier Total
Co-operative Act registered

1 KCADA 233 $ | 180 | 80

a DVCADA 64 17 34 51

3. | MCADA 70 Po; | 4 30

4 | TCADA 180 6 6 12
Total 547 37 256 293

(Source: Information furnished by CADAs)

As the State did not enact PIM Act, one time functional grant of = 1000 could
not be provided to any of the WUAs so as to enable them to operate and
maintain the schemes. Audit Scrutiny revealed that in one test checked
division*' 25 schemes out of 207 handed over schemes were inoperative due to
lack of maintenance. In the absence of any effective mechanism for
maintenance of the handed over schemes, longivity of investment could not be
assured.

The Department stated (November 2011) that a draft bill on PIM was being
formulated.

*! Kangsabati CAD Division

145


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

4.8.3.3 Collection of beneficiaries’ contribution

With a view to make the water users more involved with the CADWM
programme, the Gol has decided that 10 per cent of the cost of construction of
field channels will be collected as contribution from the water users before
execution of the work and formally incorporate the same in the accounts.

Scrutiny revealed that mandatory contributions from beneficiaries were not
collected by the two test checked CAD divisions prior to execution of the
schemes. Responsibility of realisation of the contributions was passed on to
the Pradhans of the GPs through which works were executed. In the tender
agreements it was mentioned that 10 per cent of the value of the works would
be realised by the GPs from the water users. Payment limited to 90 per cent of
the work value was made to the Pradhans by the divisions and booked in the
accounts. Thus, violating the guidelines the contributions from WUAs were
not incorporated in the accounts. Moreover, in absence of any record in the
divisions it could not be ascertained whether the contributions were actually
collected from the water users.

Meanwhile, the Department recommended (November 2011) to the MoWR.
Gol to waive 10 per cent beneficiary contribution against land donated by the
farmers and manual labours.

4.9 Conclusion

The objective of development of irrigation potential and its utilisation was not
achieved to the desired extent due to inherent deficiencies in planning,
execution and monitoring. Planning was not based on updated data and targets
were fixed without any input from field offices. The Department continued to
depend more on ground water in disregard to the Eleventh Five year plan
(2007-12) proposal. Adequate survey and investigations were not carried out
before taking up of schemes. Created IP could not be utilised optimally due to
existence of defunct schemes. Utilisation of irrigation potential of handed over
schemes was not monitored. Internal control measures were not strictly
followed. Non-observance of budgetary procedure resulted in under-utilisation
of funds. There were deficiencies in procurement, storage and accounting of
materials. Implementation of WBGWR Act, 2005 by the SWID hampered due
to inadequate infrastructure as well as human resources and lack of mass
awareness. Without ensuring the sustainability of created assets and putting
effective mechanism for their maintenance, MI schemes were handed over to
unregistered beneficiary committees. Utilisation of created IP of major
irrigation projects through CADWM programme suffered due to slow progress
and non enactment of suitable Participatory Irrigation Act.

4.10 Recommendations

e The Department needs to prepare plans based on the updated data and
feedback of performance from the implementing formations and fix
realistic targets.

146


Chapter 4-Chief Controlling Officer based audit

The Department needs to redirect its strategies to reduce excessive
dependence on ground water resources, especially in arsenic and
fluoride affected districts.

The Department should give special emphasis to revitalise the defunct
schemes for optimum utilisation of the created irrigation potential. The
Department also needs to address on the major issues like operation,
maintenance and sustainability of the handed over schemes.

SWID is to be equipped with modern equipments and infrastructure to
ensure effective implementation of the WBGWR Act, 2005.

Payment of price escalation by

Though initial technical bid of
March 1995 was cancelled
and fresh technical bid was
opened in August 1996, the
FF, 24 Parganas Highway
Division paid price escalation
with reference to March 1995
(Base month) resulting in
excess payment of
₹ 62.29 lakh to the contractor.

The Committee recommended that the
department should investigate the matter
thoroughly in order to find out the
person/persons responsible for excess
payment of हैं 62.29 lakh and recover the
same from the contractor,

2.16 of
2000-2001
Vol-2

AR

4" PAC Report
2006-2007

Municipal
Affairs

Lack of accountability in
Kolkata Municipal
Corporation.

The Committee recommended _ that
Municipal Affairs department and KMC
should undertake a joint enquiry about the
financial mismanagement of KMC to
identify the persons responsible for the
situation and to take punitive actions
against them as per the extant rules.

The Committee further desired that the
department should inform the Committee
about the actions taken against the
identified persons both in service and/or
retired from service.

3.13 of AR
2000-2001
Voll

90 PAC Report
2006-2007

Finance
(Taxation)

Unjustified printing of
ungummed and unperforated
Entertainment Tax Stamps
without considering the
willingness of the exhibitors
to use such stamps resulted in
a loss of हैं 73.14 lakh towards
cost of production and their
disposal.

It was recommended that the department
should investigate as to why the order for
printing of ungummed and unperforated
Entertainment Tax Stamps was placed in
such a large quantity at a time without
recording the reasons behind it and should
take actions against the person/persons
who would be found responsible in this
regard.

The Committee also instructed the
department to be more careful and rational
in dealing with such situations in future.

Public

Source: PAC Reports

Arbitrary recommendation
made by the Chief Engineer,
Public Works (Roads)
Directorate for acceptance of
abnormally higher rates than
that quoted by the agency in
the work of widening and
strengthening of
Calcutta-Basanti_ road at
53 KMP to 86 KMP (length
33 Kms) of South 24 Parganas
district resulted in undue
benefit of © 1.53 crore to the
agency

Considering the gravity of the matter, the
Committee recommended that the matter
be referred without delay to the Vigilance
Commissioner for thorough investigation.
The Committee also desired that the
Commission should be requested to leave
no stone unturned to divulge the facts and
thereby submit the report within three
months.
